Perturbation formulae for quenched random dynamics with applications to open systems and extreme value theory
Jason Atnip, Gary Froyland, Cecilia González-Tokman, Sandro Vaienti

Abstract
. We consider quasi-compact linear operator cocycles ℒ n ω := ℒ σ n − 1 ​ ω ∘ ⋯ ∘ ℒ σ ​ ω ∘ ℒ ω \mathcal{L}^{n}_{\omega}:=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{L}_{\sigma\omega}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega} driven by an invertible ergodic process σ : Ω → Ω \sigma:\Omega\to\Omega , and their small perturbations ℒ ω , ε n \mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n} . We prove an abstract ω \omega -wise first-order formula for the leading Lyapunov multipliers λ ω , ε = λ ω − θ ω ​ Δ ω , ε + o ⁡ ( Δ ω , ε ) \lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\omega}-\theta_{\omega}\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}+o(\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}) , where Δ ω , ε \Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon} quantifies the closeness of ℒ ω , ε \mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon} and ℒ ω \mathcal{L}_{\omega} . We then consider the situation where ℒ ω n \mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{n} is a transfer operator cocycle for a random map cocycle T ω n := T σ n − 1 ​ ω ∘ ⋯ ∘ T σ ​ ω ∘ T ω T_{\omega}^{n}:=T_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ T_{\sigma\omega}\circ T_{\omega} and the perturbed transfer operators ℒ ω , ε \mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon} are defined by the introduction of small random holes H ω , ε H_{\omega,\varepsilon} in [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] , creating a random open dynamical system. We obtain a first-order perturbation formula in this setting, which reads λ ω , ε = λ ω − θ ω ​ μ ω ​ ( H ω , ε ) + o ⁡ ( μ ω ​ ( H ω , ε ) ) , \lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\omega}-\theta_{\omega}\mu_{\omega}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})+o(\mu_{\omega}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})), where μ ω \mu_{\omega} is the unique equivariant random measure (and equilibrium state) for the original closed random dynamics. Our new machinery is then deployed to create a spectral approach for a quenched extreme value theory that considers random dynamics with general ergodic invertible driving, and random observations. An extreme value law is derived using the first-order terms θ ω \theta_{\omega} . Further, in the setting of random piecewise expanding interval maps, we establish the existence of random equilibrium states and conditionally invariant measures for random open systems via a random perturbative approach. Finally we prove quenched statistical limit theorems for random equilibrium states arising from contracting potentials. We illustrate the theory with a variety of explicit examples.



1.
Introduction

The spectral approach to studying deterministic dynamical systems
T:X\to X
on a phase space
X
, centres on the analysis of a transfer operator
\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{B}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(X)
, given by
\mathcal{L}f(y)=\sum_{x\in T^{-1}y}g(x)f(x)
, for
f
in a suitable Banach space
\mathcal{B}(X)
and for suitable weight functions
g:X\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
If the map
T
is
covering
and the weight function is
contracting
in the sense of
[
40
]
(or similarly if
\sup\log g<P(T)
as in
[
19
,
18
]
or if
\sup\log g-\inf\log g<h_{top}(T)
as in
[
31
,
14
]
), then one obtains the existence of an equilibrium state
\mu
, with associated conformal measure
\nu
, with the topological pressure
P(T)
and the density of equilibrium state
d\mu/d\nu
given by the logarithm of the leading (positive) eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\mathcal{L}
and the corresponding positive eigenfunction
h
, respectively. The map
T
exhibits an exponential decay of correlations with respect to
\nu
and
\mu
.

Keller and Liverani
[
36
]
showed that the leading eigenvalue
\lambda
and eigenfunction
h
of
\mathcal{L}
vary continuously with respect to certain small perturbations of
\mathcal{L}
.
One example of such a perturbation is the introduction of a small hole
H\subset X
.
When trajectories of
T
enter
H
, the trajectory ends; this generates an
open
dynamical system.
The set of initial conditions of trajectories that never land in
H
is the survivor set
X_{\infty}
.
For small holes, specialising to Lasota-Yorke maps of the interval, Liverani and Maume-Dechamps
[
39
]
apply the perturbation theory of
[
36
]
to obtain the existence of a unique conformal measure
\nu
and an absolutely continuous
conditionally
invariant measure
\mu
, with density
h\in\text{{\rm BV}}([0,1])
. The leading eigenvalue
\lambda
is interpretable as an
escape rate
, and the open system displays an exponential decay of correlations with respect to
\nu
and
\mu
.

To obtain finer information on the behaviour of
\lambda
with respect to perturbation size, particularly in the situation where the perturbation is not smooth, such as perturbations arising from the introduction of a hole, one requires some additional control on the perturbation.
Keller and Liverani
[
37
]
develop abstract conditions on
\mathcal{L}
and its perturbations
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
to ensure good first-order behaviour with respect to the perturbation size.
Following
[
37
]
, several authors
[
24
,
27
,
45
,
13
]
have used the Keller-Liverani
[
36
]
perturbation theory to obtain similar first-order behaviour of the escape rate with respect to the perturbation size for open systems in various settings.

This “linear response” of
\lambda
is exploited in Keller
[
35
]
to develop an elegant spectral approach to deriving an exponential extreme value law to describe likelihoods of observing extreme values from evaluating an observation function
h:X\to\mathbb{R}
along orbits of
T
.
In particular, the
N\to\infty
limiting law of

(1.1)

\nu\left(\left\{x\in X:h(T^{j}(x))\leq z_{N},j=0,\dots,N-1\right\}\right),

where the thresholds
z_{N}
are chosen so that
\lim_{N\to\infty}N\mu(\{x:h(x)>z_{N}\})\to t
for some
t>0
.
The spectral approach of
[
35
]
also provides a relatively explicit expression for the limit of (
1.1
), namely

\lim_{N\to\infty}\nu\left(\left\{x\in X:h(T^{j}(x))\leq z_{N},j=0,\dots,N-1\right\}\right)=\exp(-t\theta_{0}),

where
\theta_{0}
is the extremal index.

1.1.
Quenched perturbations of abstract operator cocycles and sequential/random dynamical systems

In the present work we begin with sequential compositions of linear operators
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{n}:=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega,0}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{L}_{\sigma\omega,0}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
,
where
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
is an invertible map on a configuration set
\Omega
.
The driving
\sigma
could also be an ergodic map on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},m)
.
We then consider a family of perturbed cocycles
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}:=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega,\varepsilon}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{L}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, where the size of the perturbation
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
is quantified by the value
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
(Definition
2.1
).
Our first main result is an abstract quenched formula (Theorem
2.2
) for the Lyapunov multipliers
\lambda_{\omega,0}
up to first order in the size of the perturbation
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
of the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
.
This quenched random formula generalises the main abstract first-order formula in
[
37
]
stated in the case of a single deterministic operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
.

Theorem A:

Suppose that assumptions (
(P1)
)-(
(P9)
) hold (see Section
2
).
If
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
then for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}=:\theta_{\omega,0}.

We then introduce random compositions of maps
T_{\omega}:X\to X
, drawn from a collection
\{T_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
.
A driving map
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},m)
creates a map cocycle
T_{\omega}^{n}:=T_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega}\circ\cdots\circ T_{\sigma\omega}\circ T_{\omega}
.
This map cocycle generates a transfer operator cocycle
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{n}:=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega,0}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{L}_{\sigma\omega,0}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
is the transfer operator for the map
T_{\omega}
.
At each iterate, a random hole
H_{\omega}\subset X
is introduced.

The existence of a random quenched equilibrium state, conformal measure, escape rates, and exponential decay of correlations is established in
[
2
]
for relatively large holes, generalising the large-hole constructions of
[
39
]
for a single deterministic map
T
to the random setting with general driving.
In contrast, our focus here is establishing a random quenched analogue of the results of
[
39
]
,
[
37
]
, and
[
35
]
discussed above.
To this end,
we let
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
be the transfer operator for the open map
T_{\omega}
with a hole
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
introduced in
X
, namely
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\mathbbold{1}_{X\setminus H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}f)
.
Our second collection of main results is a quenched formula for the derivative of
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
with respect to the sample invariant probability measure of the hole
\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})
(Theorem
4.6
),
as well as a quenched formula for the derivative of the escape rate
R_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{\omega,0})
with respect to the sample invariant probability measure of the hole
\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})
(Corollary
4.9
).

Corollary A:

If
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
is a random open system (see Section
3.1
) with

\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
and (
(C1)
)–(
(C8)
) hold (see Section
4
), then for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}/\lambda_{\omega,0}}{\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})}=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}

and

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{\omega,0})=\int_{\Omega}\log\lambda_{\omega,0}-\log\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\,dm(\omega).

In addition, if (
4.12
) holds and the
\mu_{\omega,0}
-measures of the random holes scale with
\varepsilon
according to (
4.13
), then for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{R_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{\omega,0})}{\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})}=\int_{\Omega}\left(1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}\right)\,dm(\omega).

1.2.
Quenched extreme value theory general sequential/random dynamics

To generalize to the random setting the rescaled distribution of the maxima given by (
1.1
), we now consider the (random) real-valued random observables
h_{\omega}
defined on the phase space
X

and construct a process
h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}\circ T^{j}_{\omega}
.
We are interested in determining the limiting law of

(1.3)

\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left\{x\in X:h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}(x))\leq z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N},j=0,\dots,N-1\right\}\right),

where
\{z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N}\}_{0\leq j\leq N-1}
is a collection of real-valued thresholds.
The sample probability measure
\mu_{\omega,0}
enjoys the equivariance property
T^{*}_{\omega}\mu_{\omega,0}=\mu_{\sigma\omega,0},
however the process
h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}\circ T^{j}_{\omega}
is not stationary in the probabilistic sense, which makes the theory slightly more difficult.

The first approach to non-stationary extreme value theory (EVT) was given
under convenient assumptions, by Hüsler in
[
33
,
34
]
. He was able to recover the usual extremal behaviour seen for
i.i.d. or stationary sequences under Leadbetter’s conditions
[
38
]
, namely (i)
guaranteed mixing properties for the probability
measure governing the process and (ii)
that the exceedances should appear scattered through the time period under consideration.
Hüsler’s results can not be applied in the dynamical systems setting because his uniform bounds on the control of the exceedances are not satisfied for deterministic, random, or sequential compositions of maps. The first contribution dealing explicitly with extreme value theory for random and sequential systems is the paper
[
28
]
; see also
[
26
]
for an application to point processes.
These works were an adaptation of Leadbetter’s conditions and Hüsler’s approach: let us call them the probabilistic approach to extreme value theory, to distinguish it from the spectral and perturbative approach used in the current paper.

As in the deterministic case, in order to avoid a degenerate limit distribution, one should conveniently choose the thresholds
z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N}.
Hüsler proved convergence to the Gumbel’s law if for some
0<t<\infty
we have convergence of the sum

(1.4)

\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mu_{\omega,0}\left(h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}(x))>z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N}\right)\rightarrow t.

for
m
-a.e.
\omega.

In our current framework we will additionally allow the positive number
t
to be any positive random variable in
L^{\infty}(m)
.
The nonstationary theory developed in
[
28
]
for quenched random processes, has the further restrictions that the observation function is fixed (
\omega
-independent), and the thresholds
z_{N}
(like the scaling
t
) are just real numbers, and requires the obvious restricted equivalent of (
1.4
).
In our framework the observation function
h_{\omega}
, the scaling
t_{\omega}
, and the thresholds
z_{\omega,N}
may all be random (but need not be).
We generalise and simplify the requirement (
1.4
) to

(1.5)

N\mu_{\omega,0}\left(h_{\omega}(x)>z_{\omega,N}\right)=t_{\omega}+\xi_{\omega,N},

where the scaling
t
may be a random variable
t\in L^{\infty}(m)
and the “errors”
\xi_{\omega,N}
satisfy (i)
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\xi_{\omega,N}=0
a.e., and (ii)
|\xi_{\omega,N}|\leq W<\infty
for a.e.
\omega
and all sufficiently large
N
.

We provide a more detailed discussion of the relationship between the conditions (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) at the end of section
5
.

In summary, we derive a spectral approach for a quenched random extreme value law, where the dynamics
T_{\omega}
is random, the observation functions
h_{\omega}
can be random, the thresholds controlling what is an extreme value can be random, and the scalings of the likelihoods of observing extreme values can be random, all controlled by general invertible ergodic driving.
Moreover, we obtain a formula for the explicit form of Gumbel law for the extreme value distribution.
This leads to our main extreme value theory result (stated in detail later as Theorem
5.5
):

Theorem B:

For a random open system
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
(see Section
3.1
), assuming (
(C1’)
), (
(C2)
), (
(C3)
), (
(C4’)
), (
(C5’)
), (
(C7’)
), (
(C8)
), and (
(S)
) (see Sections
4
and
5
), for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
one has

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\nu_{\omega,0}\left(x\in X:h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}(x))\leq z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N},j=0,\dots,N-1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{\omega,0}\left(x\in X:h_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}(x))\leq z_{\sigma^{j}\omega,N},j=0,\dots,N-1\right)$\hss}

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\left(-\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\theta_{\omega,0}\ dm(\omega)\right),

where
\nu_{\omega,0}
and
\mu_{\omega,0}
are the random conformal measure and the random invariant measure, respectively, for our random dynamics,
t_{\omega}
is a random scaling function, and
\theta_{\omega,0}
is an
\omega
-local extremal index.

This result generalises the spectral approach to extreme value theory in
[
35
]
, for a single map
T
, single observation function
h
, single scaling, and single sequence of thresholds.

Given a family of random holes
\mathcal{H}_{\omega,N}:=\{H_{\sigma^{j}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\}_{j\geq 0},
one can define
the first (random) hitting time to a hole, starting at initial condition
x
and random configuration
\omega
:

\tau_{\omega,\mathcal{H}_{\omega,N}}(x):=\inf\{k\geq 1,T^{k}_{\omega}(x)\in H_{\sigma^{k}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\}.

When this family of holes shrink with increasing
N
according to Condition (
(S)
) (see Section
5.3
),
Theorem B provides a description of the statistics of random hitting times, scaled by the measure of the holes (see Theorem
5.7
).

Corollary B:

For a random open system
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
(see Section
3.1
), assume (
(C1’)
), (
(C2)
), (
(C3)
), (
(C4’)
), (
(C5’)
), (
(C7’)
), (
(C8)
), and (
(S)
) (see Sections
4
and
5
).
For
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
one has

(1.6)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\tau_{\omega,\mathcal{H}_{\omega,N}}\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})>t_{\omega}\right)=\exp\left(-{\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\theta_{\omega,0}\ dm(\omega)}\right).

1.3.
Thermodynamic formalism for quenched random open systems

By assuming some additional uniformity in
\omega
on the maps
T_{\omega}
we use a recent random perturbative result
[
17
]
to obtain a complete quenched thermodynamic formalism.
The following existence result extends Theorem C of
[
39
]
, which concerned inserting a single small hole into the phase space of a single deterministic map
T
, to the situation of random map cocycles with small random holes with the random process controlled by general invertible ergodic driving
\sigma
.

Theorem C:

Suppose
that (
E1
)–(
(E9)
)
(see Section
6
)
hold for the random open system
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak 1],\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\text{{\rm BV}},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
(see Section
3.1
).
Then for each
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small there exists a unique random
T
-invariant probability measure
\mu_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
with
\supp(\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon})\subseteq X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
.
Furthermore,
\mu_{\varepsilon}
is the unique relative equilibrium state for the random open system and satisfies a forward and backward exponential decay of correlations. In addition, there exists a random absolutely continuous (with respect to
\left\{\nu_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
) conditionally invariant probability measure
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
with
\supp(\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon})\subseteq[0,1]\backslash H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
and density function
h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}([0,1])
.

For a more explicit statement of Theorem C as well as the relevant assumptions and definitions see Section
6
and Theorem
6.11
.

1.4.
Quenched limit theorems for random dynamics with contracting potentials

In Section
7
we prove some quenched limit theorems for closed random dynamics. These limit results are new for more general potentials and their associated equilibrium states. We use two approaches.
The first is based on the perturbative technique developed in
[
21
]
, which generalises the Nagaev-Guivarc’h method to random cocycles.
This technique establishes a relation between a suitable twisted operator cocycle and the distribution of the random Birkhoff sums.
As a consequence, it is possible to get quenched versions of the large deviation principle, the central limit theorem, and the local central limit theorem.
The second approach invokes the martingale techniques previously used in the quenched random setting in
[
22
]
.
We obtain the almost sure invariance principle (ASIP) for the equivariant measure
\mu_{\omega,0},
which also implies the central limit theorem and the law of iterated logarithms, a general bound for large deviations and a dynamical Borel-Cantelli lemma.

1.5.
Examples

We conclude with several explicit examples.
We start in Example
1
with the weight
1/T^{\prime}_{\omega}
for a family of random maps, random scalings, and random observations
h_{\omega}
with a common extremum location in phase space, which is a common fixed point of the
T_{\omega}
.
The special cases of a fixed map
T
on the one hand, and a fixed scaling
t
on the other, are also considered.
The same calculations can be extended to observation functions with common extrema on a periodic orbit common to all
T_{\omega}
.
Next in Example
2
we consider the more difficult case where orbits are distributed according to equilibrium states of a general geometric weight
|DT_{\omega}|^{-r}
,
using random
\beta
-maps and random observation functions
h_{\omega}
with a common extremum at
x=0
.
Example
3
investigates a fixed map
T
with random observation functions
h_{\omega}
with extrema in a shrinking neighbourhood of a fixed point of
T
, where the neighbourhood lacks the symmetry of Example
1
.
In the last example, Example
4
, we again consider random maps
T_{\omega}

with random observations
h_{\omega}
, but now the maxima of the observations are not related to fixed or periodic points of
T_{\omega}
.

Though we apply our results to the setting of random interval maps our results apply equally well to other random settings including random subshifts

[
9
,
42
]
, random distance expanding maps
[
43
]
, random polynomial systems
[
12
]
, random transcendental maps
[
44
]
. In addition, Theorem A (as well as (
1.2
) of Corollary A)
applies to sequential and semi-group settings including
[
5
,
48
]
. See Remark
6.12
for a sequential version of Theorem C.

2.
Sequential Perturbation Theorem

In this section we prove a general perturbation result for sequential operators acting on sequential Banach spaces. In particular, we will not require any measurability or notion of randomness in this section.

Suppose that
\Omega
is a set and that the map
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
is invertible.
Furthermore, we suppose that there is a family of (fiberwise) normed vector spaces
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
and dual spaces
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{*},\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{*}}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
such that for each
\omega\in\Omega
and each
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
there are operators
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
such that the following hold.

(P1)

There exists a
function
C_{1}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}}\leq C_{1}(\omega)\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}.

(P2)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
there is a functional
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{*}
, the dual space of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
,
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, and
\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\varphi_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
. Furthermore we assume that

\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1.

(P3)

There is an operator
Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
such that for each
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)=\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\cdot\varphi_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}+Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f).

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=0.

Note that assumptions (
(P2)
) and (
(P3)
) together imply that

\displaystyle\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1.

(P4)

There exists a function
C_{2}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}=C_{2}(\omega)<\infty.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
we define the quantities

(2.1)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)

and

(2.2)

\displaystyle\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\|\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{*}}.

(P5)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0.

(P6)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
such that
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
for every
\varepsilon>0
, we have that there exists a function
C_{3}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}:=C_{3}(\omega)<\infty.

Given
\omega\in\Omega
, if there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for each
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
we have that
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0
then we also have that
\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0
for each
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

(P7)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,0})=1.

(P8)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
with
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left(\left(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\left(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}\right)\right)=0.

(P9)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
with
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
we have the limit

\displaystyle q_{\omega,0}^{(k)}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)}

exists for each
k\geq 0
.

Consider the identity

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\varphi_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}))-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}))

(2.3)

\displaystyle=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right).

It then follows from (
2.3
), together with (
2.2
) and assumption (
(P4)
), that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\rvert\leq C_{2}(\omega)\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

In particular, given assumption (
(P5)
), (
2.4
) implies

(2.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\omega,0}

for each
\omega\in\Omega
.

Remark 2.1
.

Note that (
(P6)
) and (
2.4
) imply that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\left\lvert\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\rvert}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\leq C_{2}(\omega)C_{3}(\omega)<\infty.

For
n\geq 1
we define the normalized operator
\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}:=(\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}:=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\cdot\dots\cdot\lambda_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega,\varepsilon}.

In view of assumption (
(P3)
), induction gives

\displaystyle\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f)=\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\cdot\varphi_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}+Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f)

for each
n\geq 1
and all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
.
Similarly with (
2.3
), we have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}=\nu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}\left(\left(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{n}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\right)(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right).

We now arrive at the main result of this section.
We prove a differentiability result for the perturbed quantities
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the spirit of Keller and Liverani
[
37
]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that assumptions (
(P1)
)-(
(P8)
) hold.
If there is some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0
for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
then

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon},

or if (
(P9)
) holds then

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1})^{-1}q_{\omega,0}^{(k)}.

Proof.

Fix
\omega\in\Omega
.
First, we note that assumption (
(P6)
) implies that if there is some
\varepsilon_{0}
such that
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0
for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
(which implies, by assumption, that
\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=0
), then (
2.4
) immediately implies that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

Now we suppose that
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
.
Using (
(P2)
), (
(P3)
), and (
2.3
), for each
n\geq 1
and all
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})(\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\cdot\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\cdot\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}+Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})-Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}-Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}+\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}-Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right)

(2.6)

\displaystyle\quad=\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\right)

(2.7)

\displaystyle\qquad-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right).

Since

\displaystyle(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})=\varphi_{\omega,0}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}),

using a telescoping argument, the second term of (
2.6
)

D_{2}:=-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}^{n}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\right)

can be rewritten as

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}+\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}-\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

(2.8)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}+\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

(2.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right).

Reindexing, the second summand of the above calculation, and multiplying by
1
, (
2.9
), can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k+1})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

(2.10)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right).

And (
2.8
) from above can again be broken into two parts and then rewritten as

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right),

where in the second sum we have used the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}
.
Altogether using (
2.10
) and (
2.11
),
D_{2}
we can be written as

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

(2.12)

\displaystyle\quad=+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}-\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right).

Now for each
k\geq 0
we let

(2.13)

\displaystyle q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}:=\frac{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)}.

Using (
2.12
) we can continue our rephrasing of
\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})(\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon})
from (
2.6
) to get

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})(\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\quad=\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}-\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\left(1-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})^{-1}q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}-\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

(2.14)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right).

Dividing the calculation culminating in (
2.14
) by
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
on both sides gives

(2.15)

\displaystyle\nu_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0})\frac{\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\quad=1-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{-1}(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})^{-1}q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}^{-1}\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}-\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\right)

(2.17)

\displaystyle\qquad-\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-n}\omega,0}))\right).

Assumption (
(P8)
) ensures that (
2.17
) goes to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
and
n\to\infty
. Now, using (
2.2
), (
2.4
), (
(P1)
), and (
(P4)
) we bound (
2.16
) by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}-\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\right\rvert\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\|\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}C_{2}(\sigma^{-k}\omega)\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\right\rvert^{-1}\eta_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k}\right\rvert^{-1}\|\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k}(\varphi_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0})\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(C_{2}(\sigma^{-k}\omega))^{2}C_{1}^{k}(\sigma^{-k}\omega)\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\right\rvert^{-1}\eta_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k}\right\rvert^{-1}.

In view of (
(P5)
), (
(P6)
), and (
2.5
), for fixed
n
, we may continue from (
2.18
) and let
\varepsilon\to 0
to see that

(2.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(C_{2}(\sigma^{-k}\omega))^{2}C_{1}^{k}(\sigma^{-k}\omega)\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,0}\right\rvert^{-1}\eta_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}\left\lvert\lambda_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k}\right\rvert^{-1}=0.

In light of (
(P6)
), (
(P7)
), (
2.19
), and (
2.15
)–(
2.17
) together with (
(P8)
) and (
(P9)
), we see that first letting
\varepsilon\to 0
and then
n\to\infty
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}}{\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}}=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1})^{-1}q_{\omega,0}^{(k)}

as desired.
∎

In the sequel we will refer to the quantity on the right hand side of the last equation in the proof of the previous theorem by
\theta_{\omega,0}
, i.e. we set

(2.20)

\displaystyle\theta_{\omega,0}:=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1})^{-1}q_{\omega,0}^{(k)}.

3.
Random Open Systems

In this section we introduce the general setup of random open systems to which we can apply our perturbation theory from Section
2
.

We begin with a probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},m)
and an ergodic, invertible map
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
which preserves the measure
m
, i.e.

\displaystyle m\circ\sigma^{-1}=m.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
let
\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
be a closed subset of a complete metrisable space
X
such that the map

\displaystyle\Omega\ni\omega\longmapsto\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}

is a closed random set, i.e.
\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\subseteq X
is closed for each
\omega\in\Omega
and the map
\omega\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
is measurable
(see
[
16
]
), and we consider the maps

\displaystyle T_{\omega}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}.

By
T_{\omega}^{N}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}
we mean the
N
-fold composition

\displaystyle T_{\sigma^{N}\omega}\circ\dots\circ T_{\omega}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}.

Given a set
A\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}
we let

\displaystyle T_{\omega}^{-N}(A):=\left\{x\in\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}:T_{\omega}^{N}(x)\in A\right\}

denote the inverse image of
A
under the map
T_{\omega}^{N}
for each
\omega\in\Omega
and
N\geq 1
.
Now let

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}:=\bigcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left\{\omega\right\}\times\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\subseteq\Omega\times X,

and define the induced skew-product map
T:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathcal{J}_{0}
by

\displaystyle T(\omega,x)=(\sigma\omega,T_{\omega}(x)).

Let
\mathscr{B}
denote the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X
and let
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}
be the product
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega\times X
. We suppose the following:

(M1)

the map
T:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathcal{J}_{0}
is measurable with respect to
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}
.

Definition 3.1
.

A measure
\mu
on
\Omega\times X
with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}
is said to be
random measure
relative to
m
if it has marginal
m
, i.e. if

\mu\circ\pi^{-1}_{1}=m.

The disintegrations
\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
of
\mu
with respect to the partition
\left(\{\omega\}\times X\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

For every
B\in\mathscr{B}
, the map
\Omega\ni\omega\longmapsto\mu_{\omega}(B)\in X
is measurable,

(2)

For
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, the map
\mathscr{B}\ni B\longmapsto\mu_{\omega}(B)\in X
is a Borel measure.

We say that the random measure
\mu=\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is a
random probability measure
if for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
the fiber measure
\mu_{\omega}
is a probability measure. Given a set
Y=\cup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left\{\omega\right\}\times Y_{\omega}\subseteq\Omega\times X
, we say that the random measure
\mu=\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is supported in
Y
if
\supp(\mu)\subseteq Y
and consequently
\supp(\mu_{\omega})\subseteq Y_{\omega}
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
. We let
\mathcal{P}_{\Omega}(Y)
denote the set of all random probability measures supported in
Y
. We will frequently denote a random measure
\mu
by
\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
.

The following proposition from Crauel
[
16
]
, shows a random probability measure
\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
uniquely identifies a probability measure on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
16
]
, Propositions 3.3)
.

If
\left\{\mu_{\omega}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{J}_{0})
is a random probability measure on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
, then for every bounded measurable function
f:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
, the function

\Omega\ni\omega\longmapsto\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}}f(\omega,x)\,d\mu_{\omega}(x)

is measurable and

\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}\ni A\longmapsto\int_{\Omega}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}}\mathbbold{1}_{A}(\omega,x)\,d\mu_{\omega}(x)\,dm(\omega)

defines a probability measure on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
.

We assume there exists a family of Banach spaces
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
of real-valued functions on each
\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
and a family of potentials
\left\{g_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
with
g_{\omega,0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
such that the (fiberwise) transfer operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(f)(x):=\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-1}(x)}f(y)g_{\omega,0}(y),\quad f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\,x\in\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}

is well defined.
Using induction we see that iterates
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{N}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{N}\omega}
of the transfer operator are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{N}(f)(x):=\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-N}(x)}f(y)g_{\omega,0}^{(N)}(y),\quad f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\,x\in\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0},

where

g_{\omega,0}^{(N)}:=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}g_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}\circ T_{\omega}^{j}.

We let
\mathcal{B}
denote the space of functions
f:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{\omega}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
for each
\omega\in\Omega
and we define the global transfer operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
by
(\mathcal{L}_{0}f)_{\omega}(x):=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}f_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(x)
for
f\in\mathcal{B}
and
x\in\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
.
We assume the following measurability assumption:

(M2)

For every measurable function
h\in\mathcal{B}
, the map

(\omega,x)\mapsto(\mathcal{L}_{0}h)_{\omega}(x)
is measurable.

We suppose the following condition on the existence of a closed conformal measure.

(CCM)

There exists a random probability measure
\nu_{0}=\left\{\nu_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{J}_{0})
and measurable functions
\lambda_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
and
\varphi_{0}:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,0})=\lambda_{\omega,0}\varphi_{\sigma\omega,0}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(f))=\lambda_{\omega,0}\nu_{\omega,0}(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
where
\varphi_{\omega,0}(\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}):=\varphi_{0}(\omega,\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}})
. Furthermore, we suppose that the fiber measures
\nu_{\omega,0}
are non-atomic and that
\lambda_{\omega,0}:=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1})
with
\log\lambda_{\omega,0}\in L^{1}(m)
.
We then define the random probability measure

\mu_{0}
on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
by

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mu_{\omega,0}(f):=\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}}f\varphi_{\omega,0}\ d\nu_{\omega,0},\qquad f\in L^{1}(\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0},\nu_{\omega,0}).

From the definition, one can easily show that
\mu_{0}
is
T
-invariant, that is,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}}f\circ T_{\omega}\ d\mu_{\omega,0}=\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}}f\ d\mu_{\sigma\omega,0},\qquad f\in L^{1}(\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0},\mu_{\sigma\omega,0}).

Remark 3.3
.

Our Assumption (
(CCM)
) has been shown to hold in several random settings: random interval maps
[
4
,
3
]
, random subshifts
[
9
,
42
]
, random distance expanding maps
[
43
]
, random polynomial systems
[
12
]
, and random transcendental maps
[
44
]
.

We will also assume that the fiberwise Banach spaces
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\subseteq L^{\infty}(\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0},\nu_{\omega,0})
and that there exists a measurable
m
-a.e. finite function
K:\Omega\to[1,\infty)
such that

(B)

\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\leq K_{\omega}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
and each
\omega\in\Omega
, where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\omega}
denotes the supremum norm with respect to
\nu_{\omega,0}
.
We are now ready to introduce holes into the closed systems.

3.1.
Open Systems

For each
\varepsilon>0
we let
H_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{0}
be measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}
on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
such that

(A)

H_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\subseteq H_{\varepsilon}
for each
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
the sets
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
are uniquely determined by the condition that

\displaystyle\left\{\omega\right\}\times H_{\omega,\varepsilon}=H_{\varepsilon}\cap\left(\left\{\omega\right\}\times\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\right).

Equivalently we have

\displaystyle H_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\pi_{2}(H_{\varepsilon}\cap(\left\{\omega\right\}\times\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0})),

where
\pi_{2}:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
is the projection onto the second coordinate. By definition we have that the sets
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
are
\nu_{\omega,0}
-measurable, and (
(A)
) implies that

(A’)

H_{\omega,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\subseteq H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
for each
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
and each
\omega\in\Omega
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
we set

\Omega_{+,\varepsilon}:=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0\right\}

and then define

(3.3)

\displaystyle\Omega_{+}:=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\Omega_{+,\varepsilon}.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that the set
\Omega_{+}
is measurable as it is the intersection of a decreasing family of measurable sets and it is not necessarily
\sigma
-invariant.

Now define

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\backslash H_{\omega,\varepsilon},

and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left\{\omega\right\}\times\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

For each
\omega\in\Omega
,
N\geq 0
, and
\varepsilon>0
we define

\displaystyle X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}:T_{\omega}^{j}(x)\notin H_{\sigma^{j}\omega,\varepsilon}\text{ for all }0\leq j\leq N\right\}=\bigcap_{j=0}^{N}T_{\omega}^{-j}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{j}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)

to be the set of points in
\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
which survive, i.e. those points whose trajectories do not land in the holes, for
N
iterates. We then naturally define

\displaystyle X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}:=\bigcap_{N=0}^{\infty}X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon}=\bigcap_{N=0}^{\infty}T_{\omega}^{-N}(\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,\varepsilon})

to be the set of points which will never land in a hole under iteration of the maps
T_{\omega}^{N}
for any
N\geq 0
. We call
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
the
(\omega,\varepsilon)
-surviving set
.
Note that the sets
X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon}
and
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
are forward invariant satisfying the properties

(3.4)

\displaystyle T_{\omega}(X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon})\subseteq X_{\sigma\omega,N-1,\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad T_{\omega}(X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon})\subseteq X_{\sigma\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}.

Now for any
0\leq\alpha\leq\infty
we set

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\omega,\alpha,\varepsilon}:=\mathbbold{1}_{X_{\omega,\alpha,\varepsilon}}=\prod_{j=0}^{\alpha}\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{j}\omega,\varepsilon}}\circ T_{\omega}^{j}.

The global surviving set is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\alpha,\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left\{\omega\right\}\times X_{\omega,\alpha,\varepsilon}

for each
0\leq\alpha\leq\infty
.
Then
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}
is precisely the set of points that survive under forward iteration of the skew-product map
T
. We will assume that the fiberwise survivor sets are nonempty:

(X)

For each
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small we have
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

As an immediate consequence of (
(X)
) we have that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
.

Remark 3.5
.

Note that since (
(A)
) implies that
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\supseteq X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
, (
(X)
) holds if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
. Furthermore, since
T_{\omega}(X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon})\subseteq X_{\sigma\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
, if
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
then
X_{\sigma^{N}\omega,\infty,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
for each
N\geq 1
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon
. As
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}
is forward invariant we have that
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
not only implies that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
, but also that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}
is infinite.

The following proposition presents a setting in which the survivor set is nonempty for random piecewise continuous open dynamics.

Proposition 3.6
.

Suppose that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

there exist
V_{\omega},U_{\omega,1},\dots,U_{\omega,k_{\omega}}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
nonempty compact subsets such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

(1)

T_{\omega}\rvert_{U_{\omega},j}
is continuous for each
1\leq j\leq k_{\omega}
,

(2)

T_{\omega}(U_{\omega})\supseteq V_{\sigma\omega}
, where
U_{\omega}:=\cup_{j=1}^{k_{\omega}}U_{\omega,j}\subseteq V_{\omega}
for each
\omega
.

Then
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and consequently
\mathcal{X}_{\infty,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
.
Furthermore, if

m(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:k_{\omega}>1\right\})>0

then for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
the survivor set
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
is uncountable.

Proof.

For each
1\leq j\leq k_{\omega}
let
T_{\omega,j}:U_{\omega,j}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}
denote the continuous map
T_{\omega}\rvert_{U_{\omega,j}}
, and let
T_{\omega,U}:U_{\omega}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}
denote the map
T_{\omega}\rvert_{U_{\omega}}
. Since
V_{\sigma\omega}
is compact, for each
1\leq j\leq k_{\omega}
we have that
T_{\omega,j}^{-1}(V_{\sigma\omega})
is a nonempty compact subset of
U_{\omega,j}
.
Given
N\geq 1
let
\gamma=\gamma_{0}\gamma_{1}\dots\gamma_{N-1}
be an
N
-length word with
1\leq\gamma_{j}\leq k_{\sigma^{j}\omega}
for each
0\leq j\leq N-1
. Let
\Gamma_{\omega,N}
denote the finite collection of all such words of length
N
. Then for each
N\geq 1
and each
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\omega,N}

T_{\omega,\gamma}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega}):=T_{\omega,\gamma_{0}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ T_{\sigma^{N-1}\omega,\gamma_{N-1}}^{-1}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})\subseteq U_{\omega,\gamma_{0}}

is compact.
Furthermore,
T_{\omega,\gamma}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})
forms a decreasing sequence in
U_{\omega,\gamma_{0}}
. Hence, we see that

T_{\omega,U}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\omega,N}}T_{\omega,\gamma}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})

forms a decreasing sequence of compact subsets of
U_{\omega}
.
Thus,

X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}=\bigcap_{N=0}^{\infty}T_{\omega}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})\supseteq\bigcap_{N=0}^{\infty}T_{\omega,U}^{-N}(V_{\sigma^{N}\omega})\neq\emptyset

as desired. The final claim follows from the ergodicity of
\sigma
, which ensures that for a.e.
\omega
there are infinitely many
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k_{\sigma^{j}\omega}>1
, and the usual bijection between a point in
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
and an infinite word
\gamma
in the fiberwise sequence space
\Sigma_{\omega}:=\left\{\gamma=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\dots:1\leq\gamma_{j}\leq k_{\sigma^{j}\omega}\right\}
.
∎

Now we define the perturbed operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f):=\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}\left(f\cdot\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\right)=\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-1}(x)}f(y)\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}}g_{\omega,0}(y)=\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-1}(x)}f(y)g_{\omega,\varepsilon}(y),\quad f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega},

where for each
\omega\in\Omega
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
we define
g_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=g_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,\varepsilon}}
, and, similarly, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(N)}:=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}g_{\sigma^{j}\omega,\varepsilon}\circ T_{\omega}^{j}.

Note that measurability of
H_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{0}
and condition (
(M2)
) imply that for every
\varepsilon>0
and
f\in\mathcal{B}
the map
(\omega,x)\mapsto(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}f)_{\omega}(x)
is also measurable.
Iterates of the perturbed operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{N}\omega}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}:=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{N-1}\omega,\varepsilon}\circ\dots\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon},

which, using induction, we may write as

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}(f)=\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{N}\left(f\cdot\hat{X}_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon}\right),\qquad f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}.

For every
\varepsilon\geq 0
we let

\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

Definition 3.7
.

We will call a closed random dynamical system that satisfies the measurability conditions (
(M1)
), (
(M2)
), (
(CCM)
), and (
(B)
) a
random open system
if
assumptions (
(A)
) and (
(X)
) are also satisfied.
We let
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})

denote the random open system generated by the random maps
T_{\omega}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}
and random holes
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}
.

3.2.
Some of the Terms from Sequential Perturbation Theorem

In this short section we develop a more thorough understanding of some of the vital terms in the general sequential perturbation theorem of Section
2
in the setting of random open systems.
We begin by calculating the quantities
\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
and
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
from Section
2
. In particular, we have that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,0}\cdot\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}\cdot\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,0}\cdot\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}\cdot\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})

and

\displaystyle\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle=\|\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right)\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq 1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\psi\cdot\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}\cdot\sup_{\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq 1}\nu_{\omega,0}\left(\psi\cdot\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\right)

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\omega}\lambda_{\omega,0}\cdot\nu_{\omega,0}\left(H_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right),

where the last line follows from (
(B)
).

For
\varepsilon>0
and
\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0
, calculating
q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
in this setting gives

\displaystyle q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}:=\frac{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon}^{k})(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0})\right)}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,0})\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1}\cdot\mu_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\left(T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\cap\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)+j}(H_{\sigma^{-j}\omega,\varepsilon}^{c})\right)\cap H_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)}{\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1}\cdot\mu_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\left(T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\cap\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)+j}(H_{\sigma^{-j}\omega,\varepsilon}^{c})\right)\cap H_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)}{\mu_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\left(T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)}.

For notational convenience we define the quantity
\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
by

(3.8)

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}:=\frac{\mu_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\left(T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\cap\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)+j}(H_{\sigma^{-j}\omega,\varepsilon}^{c})\right)\cap H_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}\right)}{\mu_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}\left(T_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega}^{-(k+1)}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)},

and thus we have that

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}=\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1}\right)^{-1}q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}.

In light of (
3.8
), one can think of
\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
as the conditional probability (on the fiber
\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega
) of a point starting in the hole
H_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,\varepsilon}
, leaving and avoiding holes for
k
steps, and finally landing in the hole
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
after exactly
k+1
steps conditioned on the trajectory of the point landing in
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
.
Similarly, for
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
, we set

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}:=\left(\lambda_{\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega,0}^{k+1}\right)^{-1}q_{\omega,0}^{(k)}.

4.
Quenched perturbation theorem and escape rate asymptotics for random open systems

In this section we introduce versions of the assumptions (
(P1)
)–(
(P9)
) tailored to random open systems. Under these assumptions we then prove a derivative result akin to Theorem
2.2
for random open systems as well as a similar derivative result for the escape rate.

Suppose
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
is a random open system.
We assume the following conditions hold:

(C1)

There exists a measurable
m
-a.e. finite function
C_{1}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}}\leq C_{1}(\omega)\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}.

(C2)

For each
\varepsilon\geq 0
there is a random measure
\left\{\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
supported in
\mathcal{J}_{0}

and measurable functions
\lambda_{\varepsilon}:\Omega\to(0,\infty)
with
\log\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in L^{1}(m)
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\varphi_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
. Furthermore we assume that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\omega,0}(\mathbbold{1})=1.

(C3)

There is an operator
Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and each
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)=\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\cdot\varphi_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}+Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f).

Furthermore, for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=0.

(C4)

For each
f\in\mathcal{B}
there exist measurable functions
C_{f}:\Omega\to(0,\infty)
and
\alpha:\Omega\times\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
with
\alpha_{\omega}(N)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}f_{\omega}\|_{\infty,\sigma^{N}\omega}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}(\omega)\alpha_{\omega}(N)\|f_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}},

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|Q_{\sigma^{-N}\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}f_{\sigma^{-N}\omega}\|_{\infty,\omega}

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}(\omega)\alpha_{\omega}(N)\|f_{\sigma^{-N}\omega}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{-N}\omega}},

and
\|\varphi_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}\|_{\infty,\sigma^{N}\omega}\alpha_{\omega}(N)\to 0
,
\|\varphi_{\sigma^{-N}\omega,0}\|_{\infty,\sigma^{-N}\omega}\alpha_{\omega}(N)\to 0
as
N\to\infty
.

(C5)

There exists a measurable
m
-a.e. finite function
C_{2}:\Omega\to[1,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\|\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\|_{\infty,\omega}\leq C_{2}(\omega)\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq C_{2}(\omega).

(C6)

For
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0.

(C7)

There exists a measurable
m
-a.e. finite function
C_{3}:\Omega\to[1,\infty)
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small we have

\displaystyle\inf\varphi_{\omega,0}\geq C_{3}^{-1}(\omega)>0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\essinf_{\omega}\inf\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(C8)

For
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega_{+}

we have that the limit
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
exists for each
k\geq 0
, where
\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(k)}
is as in (
3.8
).

Remark 4.1
.

To obtain the scaling required in (
(C2)
) and (
(C3)
), in particular to obtain the assumption that
\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1
, suppose that
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
is a random open system satisfying the following properties:

(O1)

For each
\varepsilon\geq 0
there is a random measure
\left\{\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
with
\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{*}
, the dual space of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
,
\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, and
\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\varphi^{\prime}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
.

(O2)

There is an operator
Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{\prime}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and each
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have

\displaystyle(\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)=\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}+Q^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f).

Furthermore, for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(Q^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=0.

Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
and each
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
, by setting

\displaystyle\varphi_{\omega,0}

\displaystyle:=\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,0},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}\cdot\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\nu_{\omega,0}(f)

\displaystyle:=\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,0}(f),

\displaystyle\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,0},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})}{\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle Q_{\omega,0}(f)

\displaystyle:=Q^{\prime}_{\omega,0}(f),

\displaystyle Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})}Q^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

we see that (
(C2)
) and (
(C3)
) hold, and in particular,
\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1
. Furthermore, (
(O1)
) and (
(O2)
) together imply that
\nu^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
. Note that the measures
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}
(for
\varepsilon>0
) described in (
(C2)
) are not necessarily probability measures and should not be confused with the conformal measures for the open systems.

Now given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega\in\Omega
, for
\psi_{\omega}\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
such that
\nu_{\omega,0}(\psi_{\omega})=1
we have

\displaystyle\int_{X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon}}\psi_{\omega}\,d\nu_{\omega,0}=\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}}\psi_{\omega}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{j-N}\omega,\varepsilon}}\circ T_{\omega}^{j}\,d\nu_{\omega,0}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}\right)^{-1}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{N}\left(\psi_{\omega}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbbold{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{j-N}\omega,\varepsilon}}\circ T_{\omega}^{j}\right)\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\left(\psi_{\omega}\right)\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\psi_{\omega})\cdot\varphi_{\sigma^{N}\omega,\varepsilon}\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}+\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\left(\psi_{\omega}\right)\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}.

Thus if
\psi_{\omega}=\mathbbold{1}
we have

(4.1)

\nu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon})=\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathbbold{1})+\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\left(\mathbbold{1}\right)\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0},

and if
\psi_{\omega}=\varphi_{\omega,0}
, then we have

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon})=\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,0})+\frac{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}}Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\left(\varphi_{\omega,0}\right)\,d\nu_{\sigma^{N}\omega,0}.

Remark 4.2
.

Using (
2.3
) and (
(C7)
) we see that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,0}-\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}\nu_{\omega,0}(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\geq\lambda_{\omega,0}\nu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})\inf\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\geq 0.

Thus, we have that
\lambda_{\omega,0}\geq\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
. Note that if

\nu_{\omega,0}(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0
then we have that
\lambda_{\omega,0}>\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
. Consequently, if

\nu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0
then we have that
\lambda_{\omega,0}=\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Given a random probability measure
\zeta
on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
, for each
\varepsilon>0
, we define the lower and upper escape rates on the fiber
\omega\in\Omega
respectively by the following:

\displaystyle\underline{R}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\omega}):=-\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\zeta_{\omega}(X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon})\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{R}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\omega}):=-\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\zeta_{\omega}(X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon}).

If
\underline{R}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\omega})=\overline{R}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\omega})
we say the escape rate exists and denote the common value by
R_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\omega})
.

As an immediate consequence of (
4.1
), (
4.2
), and assumptions (
(C4)
), (
(C5)
), and (
(C7)
)
for each (fixed)
\varepsilon>0
we have that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\nu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N,\varepsilon})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}-\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}.

Since
\log\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in L^{1}(m)
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
by (
(C2)
), the following proposition follows directly from Birkhoff’s Ergodic Theorem.

Proposition 4.4
.

Given a random open system
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
satisfying conditions (
(C1)
)-(
(C7)
), for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have that

(4.4)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{\omega,0})=R_{\varepsilon}(\mu_{\omega,0})=\int_{\Omega}\log\lambda_{\omega,0}\,dm(\omega)-\int_{\Omega}\log\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}\,dm(\omega).

We now begin to work toward an application of Theorem
2.2
to random open systems.
The following implications are immediate: (
(C1)
)
\implies
(
(P1)
), (
(C2)
)
\implies
(
(P2)
), (
(C3)
)
\implies
(
(P3)
), (
(C5)
)
\implies
(
(P4)
), and in light of (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) we also have that (
(C6)
)
\implies
(
(P5)
) and (
(C7)
)
\implies
(
(P6)
).
Thus, in order to ensure that Theorem
2.2
applies for the random open dynamical setting we need only to check assumptions (
(P7)
) and (
(P8)
). We now prove the following lemma showing that (
(P7)
) and (
(P8)
) follow from assumptions (
(C1)
)-(
(C7)
).

Recall that the set
\Omega_{+}
, defined in (
3.3
), is the set of all fibers
\omega
such that
\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Given a random open system
(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak\mathcal{J}_{0},\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\mathcal{B},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
satisfying conditions (
(C1)
)-(
(C7)
), for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
we have that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,0})=1

and

(4.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{-1}\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}\left((\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})(Q_{\sigma^{-N}\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\varphi_{\sigma^{-N}\omega,0})\right)=0.

Proof.

First, using (
3.6
), we note that if
\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})>0
then so is
\Delta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
.
To prove (
4.5
), we note that for fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}}{\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}}\mu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon})=1

since
\lambda_{\omega,0}^{N}/\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{N}\to 1
(by (
2.4
), (
3.7
), (
(C5)
), and (
(C6)
)) and non-atomicity of
\nu_{\omega,0}
(
(CCM)
) together with (
(C3)
) imply that
\mu_{\omega,0}(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon})\to 1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Using (
4.2
) we can write

(4.8)
