\nu_{\omega,0}
is fully supported and non-atomic (Lemma
6.8
), Proposition
6.2
implies that

(6.13)

(1/2)\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq 2\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}

for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
.
Furthermore, applying (
6.13
) twice, we see that

(6.14)

(1/4)\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\leq 4\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}

for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It follows from the proof of Proposition
6.2
that

(6.15)

\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}=\|f\|_{\infty,1}

for all
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
and
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, where
\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\infty,\omega}
denotes the supremum norm with respect to
\nu_{\omega,0}
.
From (
6.13
) we see that (
(B)
) is clearly satisfied.

From Lemma
6.8
we have that
\lambda_{\omega,0}:=\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1})
and thus we may update (
6.8
) to get

(6.16)

\displaystyle\essinf_{\omega}\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}\geq\essinf_{\omega}\inf_{\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}\inf\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n^{\prime}}\mathbbold{1}\geq\essinf_{\omega}\inf g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}>0.

Note that since the conditions (
(B)
), (
(X)
), and (
(CCM)
) have been verified and we have assumed (
(A)
) and (
(MC)
), we see that
(\Omega,m,\sigma,[0,1],T,\text{{\rm BV}},\mathcal{L}_{0},\varphi_{0},H_{\varepsilon})
forms a random open system as defined in Section
3.1
for all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.
We now use hyperbolicity of the
\varepsilon=0
transfer operator cocycle to guarantee that we have hyperbolic cocycles for small
\varepsilon>0
, which will yield (
(C2)
), (
(C3)
), (
(C4’)
), (
(C5’)
), (
(C6)
), and (
(C7’)
) for small positive
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Assume that the conditions (
E1
)–(
(E9)
) hold for the random open system

(\Omega,\allowbreak m,\allowbreak\sigma,\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak 1],\allowbreak T,\allowbreak\text{{\rm BV}}_{1},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}_{0},\allowbreak\nu_{0},\allowbreak\varphi_{0},\allowbreak H_{\varepsilon})
.
Then for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, conditions (
(C2)
), (
(C3)
), (
(C4’)
), (
(C5’)
), (
(C6)
), and (
(C7’)
) hold.
Furthermore, the functionals
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}^{*}
can be identified with non-atomic Borel measures.

Proof.

For each
\omega
and
\varepsilon>0
we define
\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\lambda_{\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
; note that
\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}=\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
.
Our strategy is to apply Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
, to conclude that for small
\varepsilon
the cocycles
\{\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\}
are uniformly hyperbolic when considered as cocycles on the Banach space
(\text{{\rm BV}}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}})
.
Because of (
6.13
), we will conclude the existence of a uniformly hyperbolic splitting in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
for a.e.
\omega
.

First, we note that Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
assumes that the Banach space on which the transfer operator cocycle acts is separable.
A careful check of the proof of Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
shows that it holds for the Banach space
(\text{{\rm BV}}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}})
under the alternative condition (
(MC)
)
(see Appendix
B
).
To apply Theorem A
[
17
]
we require, in our notation, that:

(1)

\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
is a hyperbolic transfer operator cocycle on
\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}
with norm
\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}
and a one-dimensional leading Oseledets space (see Definition 3.1
[
17
]
), and slow and fast growth rates
0<\gamma<\Gamma
, respectively. We will construct
\gamma
and
\Gamma
shortly.

(2)

The family of cocycles
\{\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\}_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
satisfy a uniform Lasota–Yorke inequality

\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}^{k}_{\omega,\varepsilon}f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\leq A\alpha^{k}\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}+B^{k}\|f\|_{1}

for a.e.
\omega
and
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, where
\alpha\leq\gamma<\Gamma\leq B
.

(3)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\esssup_{\omega}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}=0
, where
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
is the
\text{{\rm BV}}-L^{1}(\text{{\rm Leb}})
triple norm.

By Lemma
6.8

we obtain a unique measurable family of equivariant functions
\{\varphi_{\omega,0}\}
satisfying (
(C7’)
) and (
(C5’)
) for
\varepsilon=0
.
We have the equivariant splitting
\mathrm{span}\{\varphi_{\omega,0}\}\oplus V_{\omega}
, where
V_{\omega}=\{f\in\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}:\nu_{\omega,0}(f)=0\}
.
We claim that this splitting is hyperbolic in the sense of Definition 3.1
[
17
]
; this will yield item (
1
) above.
To show this, we verify conditions (H1)–(H3) in
[
17
]
.
In our setting, Condition (H1)
[
17
]
requires the norm of the projection onto the top space spanned by
\varphi_{\omega,0}
, along the annihilator of
\nu_{\omega,0}
, to be uniformly bounded in
\omega
.
This is true because this projection acting on
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}
is
\nu_{\omega,0}(f)\varphi_{\omega,0}
and therefore

\|\nu_{\omega,0}(f)\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\leq\esssup_{\omega}\|\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\cdot\nu_{\omega,0}(f)\leq 2\esssup_{\omega}\|\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\cdot\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}},

using (
(C5’)
) and equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
(
6.13
).
Next, we define

\alpha^{n^{\prime}}:=\frac{9\esssup_{\omega}\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\essinf_{\omega}\inf_{\varepsilon\geq 0}\inf\mathcal{L}^{n^{\prime}}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1}}<1,

which is possible by (
E8
).
Condition (H2) requires
\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\omega,0}\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\geq C\Gamma^{n}\|\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}
for some
C>0
,
\Gamma>0
, all
n
and a.e.
\omega
.
By (
(C7’)
) one has

\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\omega,0}\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}=\|\varphi_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\geq\essinf_{\omega}\inf\varphi_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}>0,

and thus we obtain (H2) with
C=\Gamma=1
.
Condition (H3) requires
\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\omega,0}|_{V_{\omega}}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\leq K\gamma^{n}
for some
K<\infty
,
\alpha\leq\gamma<1
, all
n
and a.e.
\omega
.
This is provided by the
\varepsilon=0
part of (
(C4’)
)—specifically the stronger exponential version guaranteed by Lemma
6.8
—and the equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}
.

For item (
2
) we begin with the Lasota–Yorke inequality for
\text{{\rm var}}(\cdot)
and
\nu_{\omega,0}(|\cdot|)
provided by the final line of the proof of Lemma
C.1
(equation (
C.7
)).
Dividing through by
\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n^{\prime}}f)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{({n^{\prime}})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+\frac{8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}(\mathcal{A})}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|)

(6.17)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{n^{\prime}}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+\frac{\alpha^{n^{\prime}}}{\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}(\mathcal{A})}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|),

noting that
\essinf_{\omega}\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}(\mathcal{A})}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)>0
since the uniform open covering assumption (
(E9)
) together with (
E3
) and (
6.16
) imply that
and equivariance of the backward adjoint cocycle together imply that
for
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}
we have

(6.18)

\displaystyle\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)=\nu_{\sigma^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}\omega,0}\left(\left(\lambda_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}\mathbbold{1}_{Z}\right)\geq\frac{\inf g_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}}>0.

As the holes
H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
are composed of finite unions of disjoint intervals, assumption (
E6
) implies that the radii of each of these intervals must go to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus, using (
E6
) together with the fact that
\nu_{\omega,0}
is fully supported and non-atomic,
we see that (
(C6)
) must hold.

We construct a uniform Lasota–Yorke inequality for all
n
in the usual way by using blocks of length
jn^{\prime}
; we write this as

(6.19)

\text{{\rm var}}(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f)\leq A_{1}\alpha^{n}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+A_{2}^{n}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|)

for some
A_{2}>\alpha
.
We now wish to convert this to an inequality

(6.20)

\text{{\rm var}}(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f)\leq A_{1}^{\prime}\alpha^{n}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+(A_{2}^{\prime})^{n}\|f\|_{1}.

In light of (
E2
), (
6.16
), (
E1
), and (
6.5
), we see that there is a constant
B
so that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n^{\prime}}f\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}\int_{[0,1]}\left|\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-n^{\prime}}x}g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}(y)\hat{X}_{\omega,n^{\prime}-1,\varepsilon}(y)f(y)\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}J_{\omega}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}}\int_{[0,1]}\left|\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-n^{\prime}}(x)}\frac{f(y)}{J_{\omega}^{(n^{\prime})}(y)}\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}J_{\omega}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}}\int_{[0,1]}\left|P_{\omega}^{n^{\prime}}(f)\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n^{\prime})}J_{\omega}^{(n^{\prime})}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n^{\prime}}}\|f\|_{1}\leq B^{n^{\prime}}\|f\|_{1}.

Using the non-atomicicty of
\nu_{\omega,0}
from (
(CCM)
) (shown in (
6.8
)) and the fact that
\text{{\rm var}}(|f|)\leq\text{{\rm var}}(f)
, we may apply Lemma 5.2
[
10
]
to
\nu_{\omega,0}
to obtain that for each
\zeta>0
, there is a
B_{\zeta}<\infty
such that
\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|)\leq\zeta\text{{\rm var}}(f)+B_{\zeta}\|f\|_{1}
.
Now using (
6.19
) we see that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\text{{\rm var}}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f)+\|\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq A_{1}\alpha^{n}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+A_{2}^{n}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|)+B^{n}\|f\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(A_{1}\alpha^{n}+A_{2}^{n}\zeta)\text{{\rm var}}(f)+(B^{n}+A_{2}^{n}B_{\zeta})\|f\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(A_{1}\alpha^{n}+A_{2}^{n}\zeta)\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}+(B^{n}+A_{2}^{n}(B_{\zeta}-\zeta)-A_{1}\alpha^{n}))\|f\|_{1}.

Selecting
\zeta
sufficiently small and
n^{\prime\prime}
sufficiently large so that
C(\alpha^{n^{\prime\prime}}+\zeta)<1
we again (by proceeding in blocks of
n^{\prime\prime}
) arrive at a uniform Lasota–Yorke inequality of the form (
6.20
) for all
n\geq 0
.

For item (
3
) we note that

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}=1}\|(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}-\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon})f\|_{1}=\sup_{\|f\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}=1}\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(f\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})\|_{1}\leq\|\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\omega,0}^{-1}\int_{[0,1]}\left|\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-1}(x)}g_{\omega,0}(y)\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}(y)\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}J_{\omega}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}}\int_{[0,1]}\left|\sum_{y\in T_{\omega}^{-1}(x)}\frac{\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}}(y)}{J_{\omega}(y)}\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}J_{\omega}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}}\int_{[0,1]}\left|P_{\omega}(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}})\right|d\text{{\rm Leb}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\esssup_{\omega}\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}J_{\omega}\|_{\infty,1}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}}\cdot\esssup_{\omega}\mathrm{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon}).

Because (
E2
), (
E1
) and (
6.16
) imply
\esssup_{\omega}\nicefrac{{\|g_{\omega,0}J_{\omega}\|_{\infty,1}}}{{\lambda_{\omega,0}}}<\infty
, and since (
E6
) implies that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\esssup_{\omega}\mathrm{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0
, we obtain item (
3
).

We may now apply Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
to conclude that given
\delta>0
there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
the cocycle generated by
\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is hyperbolic, with

(i)

the existence of an equivariant family
\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}
with
\esssup_{\omega}\|\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon}-\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}<\delta
,

(ii)

existence of corresponding Lyapunov multipliers
\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
satisfying
|\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon}-1|<\delta
,

(iii)

operators
\hat{Q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
satisfying
\|(\hat{Q}_{\omega,\varepsilon})^{n}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}\leq K^{\prime}(\gamma+\delta)^{n}
, where
\gamma
is the decay rate for
Q_{\omega,0}
from the proof of Lemma
6.8
.

To obtain an
\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{*}
-equivariant family of linear functionals
\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}^{*}
we apply Corollary 2.5
[
23
]
.
Using the one-dimensionality of the leading Oseledets space for the forward cocycle, this result shows
that the leading Oseledets space for the backward adjoint cocycle is also one-dimensional.
This leading Oseledets space is spanned by some
\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}^{*}
, satisfying
\hat{\nu}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f))=\hat{}\vartheta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)
, for Lyapunov multipliers
\hat{}\vartheta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma 2.6
[
23
]
, we may scale the
\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
so that
\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1
for a.e.
\omega
.
We show that in fact
\hat{}\vartheta_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon}

m
-a.e.
Indeed,

1=\hat{\nu}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\hat{\varphi}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})=\hat{\nu}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon}/\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=(\hat{}\vartheta_{\omega,\varepsilon}/\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon})\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})=\hat{}\vartheta_{\omega,\varepsilon}/\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\omega,0}\hat{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, and we now define
\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon},\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon},Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, and
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}
by the following:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}\cdot\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})\hat{\nu}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{\omega,0}\frac{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})}{\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}\hat{}\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon})}\hat{Q}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

Clearly all of the properties of (
(C2)
) and (
(C3)
) are now satisfied except for the log-integrability of
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
in (
(C2)
).
To demonstrate this last point, we note that by uniform hyperbolicity of the perturbed cocycles,
\lambda^{\prime}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
are uniformly bounded below and are therefore log-integrable.
Since

\displaystyle|1-\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})|=|\nu_{\omega,0}(\varphi_{\omega,0})-\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})|\leq\|\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon}-\varphi_{\omega,0}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}<\delta,

the
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
are uniformly small perturbations of the
\hat{\lambda}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, and since
\lambda_{\omega,0}
is
\log
-integrable by (
E2
) and (
6.16
), we must therefore have that the log integrability condition on
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
in (
(C2)
) is satisfied.
Point (
(i)
) above, combined with the
\varepsilon=0
part of (
(C5’)
) (resp. (
(C7’)
)) and the uniform estimate for
|1-\nu_{\omega,0}(\hat{\varphi}_{\omega,\varepsilon})|
, immediately yields the
\varepsilon>0
part of (
(C5’)
) (resp. (
(C7’)
)).
Point (
(iii)
) above combined with the same estimates also ensures that the norm of
\|Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{\infty,1}
decays exponentially fast, uniformly in
\omega
and
\varepsilon
, satisfying the stronger exponential version of (
(C4’)
). In fact point (
(iii)
) implies the stronger statement that
\|Q_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}}
decays exponentially fast, uniformly in
\omega
and
\varepsilon
.

Finally we show that
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}:C^{0}([0,1])\to\mathbb{C}
is a positive linear functional with
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\in\mathbb{R}
if
f
is real.
From this fact it will follow by Riesz-Markov (e.g. Theorem A.3.11
[
49
]
) that
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}
can be identified with a real finite Borel measure on
[0,1]
.
By linearity we may consider the two cases: (i)
f=\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}>0
(the generator of the leading Oseledets space) and (ii)
f\in F_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, where
F_{\omega,\varepsilon}
is the Oseledets space complementary to
\mathrm{span\{\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}\}}
.
In case (i)
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1>0
.
In case (ii), Lemma 2.6
[
23
]
implies
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)=0
.
In summary we see that
\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}
is positive.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

As we have just shown that assumptions (
(C1’)
), (
(C2)
), (
(C3)
), (
(C4’)
), (
(C5’)
), (
(C6)
), (
(C7’)
) (Lemmas
6.8
and
6.9
), we see that Proposition
4.4
holds as well as Theorem
4.6
for the random open system
(\Omega,m,\sigma,[0,1],T,\text{{\rm BV}},\mathcal{L}_{0},\nu_{0},\varphi_{0},H_{\varepsilon})
under the additional assumption of (
(C8)
).
In light of Remark
5.3
, if we assume (
(S)
) in addition to (
(C8)
) then both Corollary
4.9
and Theorem
5.5
apply.

The following theorem is the main result of this section and elaborates on the dynamical significance of the perturbed objects produced in Lemma
6.9
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Suppose
(\Omega,m,\sigma,[0,1],T,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{L}_{0},\nu_{0},\varphi_{0},H_{\varepsilon})
is a random open system and that the assumptions of Lemma
6.9
hold.
Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that for every
0\leq\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
we have the following:

(1)

There exists a unique random probability measure
\zeta_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
on
[0,1]
such that, for
\varepsilon>0
,
\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
is supported in
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
and

\displaystyle\zeta_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}f)=\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f),

for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and each
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
, where

\displaystyle\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=\zeta_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\mathbbold{1}).

Furthermore, for
\varepsilon=0
we have
\zeta_{\omega,0}=\nu_{\omega,0}
and
\rho_{\omega,0}=\lambda_{\omega,0}
and for
\varepsilon>0
we have that
C_{1}^{-1}\leq\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\leq C_{1}
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

(2)

There exists a measurable function
h_{\varepsilon}:\Omega\times[0,1]\to(0,\infty)
such that
\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h_{\omega,\varepsilon})=1
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}h_{\omega,\varepsilon}=\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}h_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}

for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.
Moreover,
h_{\varepsilon}
is unique modulo
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
, and there exists
C\geq 1
such that
C^{-1}\leq h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\leq C
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
. Furthermore, for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have that
\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\to\rho_{\omega,0}=\lambda_{\omega,0}
and

h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\to h_{\omega,0}=\varphi_{\omega,0}
(in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
\varphi_{\omega,0}
and
\lambda_{\omega,0}
are defined in Lemma
6.8
.

(3)

The random measure
\mu_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}:=h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is a
T
-invariant and ergodic random probability measure whose fiberwise support, for
\varepsilon>0
, is contained in
X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
.
Furthermore,
\mu_{\varepsilon}
is the unique relative equilibrium state, i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\log\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\ dm(\omega)

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{E}P_{\varepsilon}(\log g_{0})=h_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}(T)+\int_{\mathcal{J}_{0}}\log g_{0}\,d{\mu_{\varepsilon}}=\sup_{\eta_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{P}_{T,m}^{H_{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{J}_{0})}h_{\eta_{\varepsilon}}(T)+\int_{\mathcal{J}_{0}}\log g_{0}\,d\eta_{\varepsilon},

where
h_{\eta_{\varepsilon}}(T)
is the entropy of the measure
\eta_{\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{E}P_{\varepsilon}(\log g_{0})
is the expected pressure of the weight function
g_{0}=\left\{g_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{T,m}^{H_{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{J}_{0})
denotes the collection of
T
-invariant random probability measures
\eta_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathcal{J}_{0}
whose disintegration
\{\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
satisfies
\eta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon})=0
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
. Furthermore,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{E}P_{\varepsilon}(\log g_{0})=\mathcal{E}P_{0}(\log g_{0}):=\int_{\Omega}\log\lambda_{\omega,0}\,dm.

(4)

For
\varepsilon>0
, let
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
be the random probability measure with fiberwise support in
[0,1]\backslash H_{\omega,\varepsilon}
whose disintegrations are given by

\displaystyle\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f):=\frac{\nu_{\omega,0}\left(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{c}}h_{\omega,\varepsilon}f\right)}{\nu_{\omega,0}\left(\mathbbold{1}_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{c}}h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\right)}

for all
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
.
\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}
is the unique random conditionally invariant probability measure that is absolutely continuous (with respect to
\left\{\nu_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
) with density of bounded variation.

(5)

For each
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
there exists
D>0
and
\kappa_{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\|\left(\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f-\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)h_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\leq D\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}.

Furthermore, for all
A\in\mathscr{B}
and
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nu_{\omega,0}\left(T_{\omega}^{-n}(A)\,\rvert\,X_{\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)-\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(A)\right\rvert\leq D\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n},

and

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}\left(f\rvert_{X_{\omega,n,\varepsilon}}\right)}{\varrho_{\omega,\varepsilon}(X_{\omega,n,\varepsilon})}-\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\right\rvert\leq D\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}.

In addition, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\kappa_{\varepsilon}=\kappa_{0}
, where
\kappa_{0}
is defined in Lemma
6.8
.

(6)

There exists
C>0
such that for every
f,h\in\text{{\rm BV}}
,
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, and for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left(f\circ T_{\omega}^{n}\right)h\right)-\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h)\right\rvert\leq C\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}.

Proof.

First we note that the claims of items (
1
) – (
3
) and (
5
) – (
6
) above for
\varepsilon=0
follow immediately from Lemma
6.8
. Now we are left to prove each of the claims for
\varepsilon>0
.

Claims (
1
) – (
3
) follow from Lemma
6.9
with the scaling:

\displaystyle h_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon})\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)}{\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathbbold{1})},

\displaystyle\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathbbold{1})}{\nu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathbbold{1})}\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}.

The uniform boundedness on
\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}
and
h_{\omega,\varepsilon}
follows from the uniform boundedness on
\lambda_{\omega,\varepsilon}
and
\varphi_{\omega,\varepsilon}
coming from Lemma
6.9
.
The fact that
\mu_{\varepsilon}
is the unique relative equilibrium state follows similarly to the proof of Theorem 2.23 in
[
4
]
(see also Remark 2.24, Lemma 12.2 and Lemma 12.3).
The claim that
\supp(\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon})\subseteq X_{\omega,\infty,\varepsilon}
follows similarly to Lemma 11.11 of
[
2
]
. Noting that
\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}=\|f\|_{\infty,1}
for
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}
by the proof of Proposition
6.2
, we now proof Claims (
4
) – (
6
).

Claim (
4
) follows from Lemma 3.5
[
2
]
and the uniqueness of the density
h_{\omega,\varepsilon}\in\text{{\rm BV}}
.

The first item of Claim (
5
) follows from Lemma
6.9
and the remaining items are proved similarly to Corollary 12.8
[
2
]
.

Claim (
6
)
follows from Claim (
5
) and is proven in Appendix
D
.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

If one considers a two-sided (bi-infinite) sequential analogue of random open systems, then because Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
also applies to two-sided sequential systems, one could prove similar results to items (
1
), (
2
), (
4
), (
5
), and (
6
) of Theorem
6.11
.

7.
Limit theorems

In this section we prove a few limit theorems for the
closed
systems discussed in Section
6

(\Omega,m,\sigma,[0,1],T,\text{{\rm BV}},\mathcal{L}_{0},\nu_{0},\varphi_{0})
. We will in fact show that such systems are
admissible
in the sense of
[
21
]
. This will allow us to adapt to our setting the spectral approach
à la Nagaev-Guivarc’h
developed in
[
21
]
and get a quenched central limit theorem, a quenched large deviation theorem and a quenched local central limit theorem. We will also present an alternative approach based on martingale techniques
[
22
,
1
,
20
]
, which produces an almost sure invariance principle (ASIP) for the random measure
\mu_{0}=\left\{\mu_{\omega,0}\right\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
. Moreover, the ASIP implies that
\mu_{0}
satisfies the central limit theorem as well as the law of the iterated logarithm. The martingale approach will also give an upper bound for any (large) deviation from the expected value and a Borel-Cantelli dynamical lemma. At the moment we could not extend the previous limit theorems to the
open
systems investigated in Section
6
. There are a few reasons for that which concern the Banach space
\mathcal{B}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
associated to those systems and defined by the norm:
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega,\varepsilon}}=\text{var}(\cdot)+\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}(|\cdot|).
First of all, we do not know if the random cocycle
\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega,\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,\varepsilon})
is quasi-compact which is an essential requirement for admissibility.
Second, the results of Theorem
6.11
are not particularly compatible with the Banach spaces
\mathcal{B}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon>0
, since the inequalities of items (
5
) and (
6
) are in terms of the norms
\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\infty,\omega}
and
\|\makebox[4.30554pt]{{$\cdot$}}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
which are defined modulo
\nu_{\omega,0}
and the
\mathcal{B}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
norm is defined via
\zeta_{\omega,\varepsilon}
, a measure which is supported on a
\nu_{\omega,0}
-null set.

7.1.
The Nagaev-Guivarc’h approach

The paper
[
21
]
developed a general scheme to adapt the Nagaev-Guivarc’h approach to random quenched dynamical systems, allowing one to prove limit theorems by exploring the connection between a
twisted
operator cocycle and the distribution of the Birkhoff sums. The results in
[
21
]
were confined to the geometric potential
|\det(DT_{\omega})|^{-1}
and the associated conformal measure, Lebesgue measure. We now show how to extend those results to the systems verifying the assumptions stated in Section
6
and the results of Theorem
6.11
, whenever
\varepsilon=0,
that is we will consider random closed systems for a larger class of potentials.

The starting point is to replace the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
associated to the geometric potential and the (conformal) Lebesgue measure introduced in
[
21
]
, with our operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
and the associated conformal measures
\nu_{\omega,0}.
In particular, if we work with the normalized operator
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}:=\lambda_{\omega,0}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
the results in
[
21
]
are reproducible almost verbatim with a few precautions which we are going to explain.
As before, let
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
be the Banach space defined by
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}=\text{var}(\cdot)+\nu_{\omega,0}(|\cdot|),
where the variation is defined using equivalence classes mod-
\nu_{\omega,0}.

In order to apply the theory in
[
21
]
we must show that our random cocycle is
admissible
. This reduces to check two sets of properties which were listed in
[
21
]
respectively as conditions (V1) to (V9) and conditions (C0) up to (C4). The first set of conditions reproduces the classical properties of the total variation of a function and its relationship with the
L^{1}(\text{{\rm Leb}})
norm. We should emphasize that in our case the variation is defined using equivalence classes mod-
\nu_{\omega,0}.

It is easy to check that properties (V1, V2, V3, V5, V8, V9) hold with respect to this variation. In particular (V3) asserts that for any
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
we have
||f||_{L^{\infty}(\nu_{\omega,0})}\leq||f||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}};
we will refer to it in the following just as the (V3) property.

Notation
: recall that given an element
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
, the
L^{\infty}
norm of
f
with respect to the measure
\nu_{\omega,0}
is denoted by
||f||_{\infty,\omega}
in the rest of this section.

Property (V7) is not used in the current paper; (V6) is a general density embedding result proved in Hofbauer and Keller (Lemma 5
[
32
]
).
We elaborate on property (V4). To obtain (V4) in our situation one needs to prove that the unit ball of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
is compactly injected into
L^{1}(\nu_{\omega,0})
. As we will see, this is used to get the quasi-compactness of the random cocycle. The result follows easily by adapting Proposition 2.3.4 in
[
11
]
to our conformal measure
\nu_{\omega,0},
which is fully supported and non-atomic.
We now rename the other set of properties (C0)–(C4) in
[
21
]
as (
\mathcal{C}
0) to (
\mathcal{C}
4) to distinguish them from our (C) properties stated earlier in Sections
4
and
5
.

•

Assumption (
\mathcal{C}
0) coincides with our condition (
(MC)
).

•

Condition (
\mathcal{C}
1) requires us to prove in our case that

(7.1)

||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}f||_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}}\leq K||f||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}},

for every
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
and for
m
-a.e.
\omega
, with
\omega
-independent
K
.

•

Condition (
\mathcal{C}
2) asks that there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
and measurable
\tilde{\alpha}^{N},\tilde{\beta}^{N}:\Omega\rightarrow(0,\infty),
with
\int_{\Omega}\log\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)dm(\omega)<0,
such that for every
f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
and
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega,

(7.2)

||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{N}_{\omega,0}f||_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{N}\omega}}\leq\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)||f||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}+\tilde{\beta}^{N}(\omega)||f||_{L^{1}(\nu_{\omega,0})}.

•

Condition (
\mathcal{C}
3) is the content of the first display equation in item
5
in the statement of Theorem
6.11
.

•

Condition (
\mathcal{C}
4) is only used to obtain Lemma 2.11 in
[
21
]
. There are three results in that Lemma which we now compare with our situation. The third result is the decay of correlations stated in item
6
of Theorem
6.11
. The second result is the almost-sure strictly positive lower bound for the density
\varphi_{\omega,0}
stated in item
2
of Theorem
6.11
. The first result requires that
\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}||\varphi_{\omega,0}||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}<\infty.
This follows by checking that the proof of Proposition 1 in
[
22
]
works in our current setting with the obvious modifications; we note that Proposition 1
[
22
]
only assumes conditions (
\mathcal{C}
1) and (
\mathcal{C}
3).

We are thus left with showing conditions (
\mathcal{C}
1) and (
\mathcal{C}
2) in our setting. We will get both at the same time as a consequence of the following argument, which consists in adapting to our current situation the final part
of Lemma
C.1
. Our starting point will be the inequality (
C.6
).
Since we are working with the normalized operator cocycle
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{N}
, we have to divide the expression in (
C.6
) by
\lambda^{n^{\prime}}_{\omega,0};
moreover we have to replace the measure
\nu_{\omega,0}
with the equivalent conformal probability measure
\nu_{\omega,0}.

Therefore (
C.6
) now becomes

(7.3)

(\lambda_{\omega,0}^{n})^{-1}\text{{\rm var}}\left(\mathbbold{1}_{T_{\omega}^{n}(Z)}\left((fg_{\omega,0}^{(n)})\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}\right)\right)\leq 9\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\lambda^{n}_{\omega,0}}\text{var}_{Z}(f)+\frac{8\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\lambda^{n}_{\omega,0}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|_{Z}),

where
Z
is an element of
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}_{\omega,0}.
Since each element of the partition
\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}_{\omega,0}
has nonempty interior and the measure
\nu_{\omega,0}
charges open intervals, if we set

\overline{\nu}^{(n)}_{\omega,0}:=\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)>0,

and we take the sum over the
Z\in\mathcal{Z}^{(n)}_{\omega,0}
we finally get

(7.4)

\text{var}(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{\omega,0}f)\leq 9\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\lambda^{n}_{\omega,0}}\text{var}(f)+8\frac{\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|)}{\lambda^{n}_{\omega,0}\overline{\nu}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}}.

Notice that condition (
\mathcal{C}
2) requires that
\tilde{\alpha}^{N}<1
, which in our case becomes

(7.5)

9\frac{\esssup_{\omega}\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\essinf_{\omega}\lambda^{n}_{\omega,0}}<1,

or equivalently

9\frac{\esssup_{\omega}\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\essinf_{\omega}\inf\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1}}<1,

which is guaranteed by (E8).
We now move on to check condition (
\mathcal{C}
1).
From (
7.4
), setting
n=1
we see that a sufficient condition for (
\mathcal{C}
1) is

(7.6)

\frac{\esssup_{\omega}\|g_{\omega,0}^{(1)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\essinf_{\omega}\lambda^{1}_{\omega,0}\overline{\nu}_{\omega,0}}<\infty.

or equivalently

(7.7)

\frac{\esssup_{\omega}\|g_{\omega,0}^{(1)}\|_{\infty,\omega}}{\essinf_{\omega}\inf\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1}\overline{\nu}_{\omega,0}}<\infty

Condition (
7.7
) is in principle checkable (
n=1
) and we could
assume
it as a part of the admissibility condition for our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}).

The admissibility conditions stated in
[
21
]
, in particular (
\mathcal{C}
2) and (
\mathcal{C}
3), were sufficient to prove the quasi-compactness of the random cocycle introduced in
[
21
]
, but they rely on another assumption, which was a part of the Banach space construction, namely that
\text{{\rm BV}}_{1}
was compactly injected into
L^{1}(\text{Leb}).
We saw above that the same result holds for our Banach space
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
and our measure
\nu_{\omega,0}.
In order to prove quasi-compactness following Lemma 2.1 in
[
21
]
, we use condition
(
\mathcal{C}
2) with the almost-sure bound
\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)<1.

We must additionally prove that the top Lyapunov exponent
\Lambda(\mathcal{R})
defined by the limit

\Lambda(\mathcal{R})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{n}||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
is not less than zero.
This will follow by applying item
5
and the bound on the density
\varphi_{\omega,0}
in item
2
, both in Theorem
6.11
.
Since the density
\varphi_{\omega,0}
is for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
bounded from below by the constant
C^{-1},
we have that
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|\varphi_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\geq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log C^{-1}=0
.
Now we continue as in the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
21
]
. Let
N
be the integer for which

\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)<1

\omega
a.s. We then consider the cocycle
\mathcal{R}_{N}
generated by the map
\omega\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{N}.
It is easy to verify that

\Lambda(\mathcal{R}_{N})=N\Lambda(\mathcal{R})
and the index of compactness

\kappa
(see section 2.1 in
[
21
]
for the definition)
of the two cocycles also satisfies

\kappa(\mathcal{R}_{N})=N\kappa(\mathcal{R}).
Because
\int\log\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)\ dm<0\leq\Lambda(\mathcal{R}_{N})
, Lemma 2.1 in
[
21
]
guarantees that
\kappa(\mathcal{R}_{N})\leq\int\log\tilde{\alpha}^{N}(\omega)\ dm
,
proving
quasicompactness of
\mathcal{R}.

As we said at the beginning of this section, we could now follow almost verbatim the proofs in
[
21
]
by using our operators
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
and by replacing the Lebesgue measure with the conformal measures
\nu_{\omega,0}.
We briefly sketch the main steps of the approach; we first define the
observable

v
as a measurable map
v:\Omega\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with the additional properties
that
||v(\omega,x)||_{L^{1}(\nu_{0})}<\infty
and

\text{ess}\sup_{\omega}||v_{\omega}||_{\infty,\omega}<\infty,
where we set
v_{\omega}(x)=v(\omega,x).

Moreover we assume
v_{\omega}
is fibrewise centered:
\int v_{\omega}(x)d\mu_{\omega,0}(x)=0,
for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega.
We then define the twisted (normalized) transfer operator cocycle as:

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{\theta}(f)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}(e^{\theta v_{\omega}(\cdot)}f),\ f\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}.

The link with the limit theorems is provided by the following equality which follows easily by the duality property of the operator:

\int\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{\theta,n}(f)d\nu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}=\int e^{\theta S_{n}v_{\omega}(\cdot)}fd\nu_{\omega,0},

where
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{\theta,n}=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma^{n-1}\omega,0}^{\theta}\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{\theta}.
The adaptation of Theorem 3.12 in
[
21
]
to our case, shows that the twisted operator is quasi-compact (in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
) for
\theta
close to zero. Moreover, by denoting with
\Lambda(\theta)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{\theta,n}||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
the top Lyapunov exponent of the cocycle, the map
\theta\rightarrow\Lambda(\theta)
is of class
C^{2}
and strictly convex in a neighborhood of zero. We have also the analog of Lemma 4.3 in
[
21
]
, linking the asymptotic behavior of characteristic functions associated to Birkhoff sums with
\Lambda(\theta):

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\left|\int e^{\theta S_{n}v_{\omega}(x)}d\mu_{\omega,0}\right|=\Lambda(\theta).

We are now ready to collect our results on a few limit theorems; we first define the variance
\Sigma^{2}=\int v_{\omega}(x)^{2}\ d\mu_{\omega,0}\ dm+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int v_{\omega}(x)v(T_{\omega}^{n}(x))\ d\mu_{\omega,0}\ dm.

We also define the
aperiodicity condition
by asking that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and for every compact interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},
there exists
C(\omega)>0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
such that
||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}^{it,n}||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq C(\omega)\rho^{n},
for
t\in J
and
n\geq 0.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible and take the centered observable
v
verifying
\text{ess}\sup_{\omega}||v_{\omega}||_{\infty,\omega}<\infty.
Then:

•

(
Large deviations
). There exists
\varkappa_{0}>0
and a non-random function
c:(-\varkappa_{0},\varkappa_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which is nonnegative, continuous, strictly convex, vanishing only at
0
and such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mu_{\omega,0}(S_{n}v_{\omega}(\cdot)>n\varkappa)=-c(\varkappa),\ \text{for}\ 0<\varkappa<\varkappa_{0},\ \text{and}\ m-\text{a.e.}\ \omega\in\Omega.

•

(
Central limit theorem
). Assume that
\Sigma^{2}>0.
Then for every bounded and continuous function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
m
-
a.e.

\omega\in\Omega
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int\varphi\left(\frac{S_{n}v_{\omega}(x)}{\sqrt{n}}\right)d\mu_{\omega,0}=\int\varphi\ d\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{2}).

•

(
Local central limit theorem
). Suppose the aperiodicity condition holds. Then for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and every bounded interval
J\subset\mathbb{R},
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\Sigma\sqrt{n}\mu_{\omega,0}(s+S_{n}v_{\omega}(\cdot)\in J)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2n\Sigma^{2}}}|J|\right|=0.

7.2.
The martingale approach

The deviation result quoted above allows to control, asymptotically, deviations of order
\varkappa,
for
\varkappa
in a sufficiently small bounded interval around
0.
We now show how to extend that result to any
\varkappa
by getting an exponential bound on the deviation of the distribution function instead of an asymptotic expansion; in particular our bound shows that deviation probability stays small for finite
n
,which is a typical concentration property.
We now derive this result since it will provide us with the martingale that is used to obtain the ASIP.
Recall that the equivariant measure
\mu_{\omega,0}
is equivalent to
\nu_{\omega,0}.
We consider again the fibrewise centered observable
v
from the previous section, and
we wish to estimate

\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|>\varkappa\right).

We will use the following result (Azuma,
[
7
]
):
Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of martingale differences.
If there is
a>0
such that
||M_{i}||_{\infty}<a
for all
i,
then we have
for all
b\in\mathbb{R}:

\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\geq nb\right)\leq e^{-n\frac{b^{2}}{2a^{2}}}.

If we denote
\mathcal{F}^{k}_{\omega}:=(T^{k}_{\omega})^{-1}(\mathcal{F}),

we can easily prove that for a measurable map

\varphi:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{{R}},
we have (the expectations
\mathbb{E}_{\omega}

will be
taken with respect to
\mu_{\omega,0}
):

(7.8)

\mathbb{E}_{\omega}(\varphi\circ T^{l}_{\o}|\mathcal{F}^{n}_{\omega})=\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma^{l}\omega,0}^{n-l}(\lambda_{\sigma^{l}\omega,0}\varphi)}{\lambda_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}}\right)\circ T_{\omega}^{n}

We now set:

M_{n}:=v_{\sigma^{n}\omega}+G_{n}-G_{n+1}\circ T_{\sigma^{n}\omega},

with
G_{0}=0
and

(7.9)

G_{n+1}=\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}(v_{\sigma^{n}\omega}\lambda_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}+G_{n}\lambda_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0})}{\lambda_{\sigma^{k+1}\omega,0}}

It is easy to check that

\mathbb{E}_{\omega}(M_{n}\circ T_{\omega}^{n}|\mathcal{F}^{n+1}_{\omega})=0,

which means that the sequence
(M_{n}\circ T_{\omega}^{n})
is a reversed martingale difference with respect to the filtration
\mathcal{F}^{n}_{\omega}.

By iterating (
7.9
) we get

(7.10)

G_{n}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{(n-j)}_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}(v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}\lambda_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}),

and by a telescopic trick we have

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}M_{k}\circ T_{\omega}^{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}T_{\omega}^{n}-G_{n}\circ T_{\omega}^{n}.

Suppose for the moment we could bound
G_{n}
uniformly in
n
, but not necessarily in
\omega
, by
||G_{n}||_{\infty,\omega}\leq C_{1}(\omega).
Since by assumption there exists a constant
C_{2}
such that
\text{ess}\sup_{\omega}||v_{\omega}||_{\infty,\omega}\leq C_{2},
we have
||M_{n}||_{\infty,\omega}\leq C_{2}+2C_{1}({\omega}),
and by Azuma:

\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}M_{k}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|>\frac{\varkappa}{2}\right)\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{8(C_{2}+2C_{1}({\omega}))^{2}}n\right\}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|>\varkappa\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}M_{k}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|+\frac{1}{n}C_{1}(\omega)>\varkappa\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}M_{k}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|>\frac{\varkappa}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{8(C_{2}+2C_{1}({\omega}))^{2}}n\right\},

provided
n>n_{0},
where
n_{0}
verifies
\frac{1}{n}_{0}C_{1}(\omega)\leq\frac{\varkappa}{2}.

In order to estimate
C_{1},
we proceed in the following manner. We have

(7.11)

||G_{n}||_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{\sigma^{n}\omega,0}}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}||\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{(n-j)}_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}(v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}\lambda_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0})||_{\infty,\omega}.

The multiplier
\lambda_{\omega,0}
is bounded from above and from below
\omega
a.s. respectively by, say,
U
and
1/U
by conditions
(\mathcal{C}1)
and item
1
Theorem
6.11
, respectively. Then we use item
5
of Theorem
6.11
and property (V3) to bound the term into the sum. In conclusion we get:

||G_{n}||_{\infty,\omega}\leq U^{2}DC_{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\kappa^{n-j}:=C_{1}.

We summarize this result in the following

Theorem 7.2
.

(
Large deviations bound
)

Suppose that our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible and take the fibrewise centered observable
v
satisfying
\text{ess}\sup_{\omega}||v_{\omega}||_{\infty,\omega}=C_{2}<\infty.
Then there exists
C_{1}>0
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

and for any
\varkappa>0:

\mu_{\omega,0}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}\circ T^{k}_{\omega}\right|>\varkappa\right)\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{8(C_{2}+2C_{1})^{2}}n\right\},

provided
n>n_{0},
where
n_{0}
is any integer number satisfying
\frac{1}{n_{0}}C_{1}\leq\frac{\varkappa}{2}.

We point out that whenever our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible, we satisfy all the assumptions in the paper
[
22
]
, which allows us to get the almost sure invariance principle. We should simply replace the (conformal) Lebesgue measure with our random conformal measure
\nu_{\omega,0},
and use our operators
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0},
as we already did in Section
7.1
for the Nagaev-Guivarc’h approach. It is worthwhile to observe that
[
22
]
relies on the construction of a suitable martingale, which in our case is precisely the reversed martingale difference
M_{n}\circ T^{n}_{\omega}
obtained above in the proof of the large deviation bound. We denote with
\Sigma^{2}_{n}
the variance
\Sigma^{2}_{n}=\mathbb{E}_{\omega}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}\circ T_{\omega}^{k}\right)^{2},
where
v_{\omega}
is a centered observable, and with
\Sigma^{2}
the quantity introduced above, in the statement of the central limit theorem. We have the following:

Theorem 7.3
.

(
Almost sure invariance principle
)

Suppose that our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible and consider a centered observable
v_{\omega}.
Then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\Sigma^{2}_{n}=\Sigma^{2}.
Moreover one of the following cases hold:

(i)
\Sigma=0,
and this is equivalent to the existence of
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mu_{0})

v=\varphi-\varphi\circ T,

where
T
is the induced skew product map and
\mu
its invariant measure, see Definition
3.1
.

(ii)
\Sigma^{2}>0
and in this case for
m
a.e.
\omega\in\Omega,

\forall\varrho>\frac{5}{4}
, by enlarging the probability space
(X,\mathcal{F},\mu_{\omega,0})
if necessary, it is possible to find a sequence
(B_{k})_{k}
of independent centered Gaussian random variables such that

\sup_{1\leq k\leq n}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(v_{\sigma^{i}\omega}\circ T_{\omega}^{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\right|=O(n^{1/4}\log^{\varrho}(n)),\ \mu_{\omega,0}\ \text{a.s.}.

We show in Section
5.47
that the distribution of the first hitting random time in a decreasing sequence of holes follows an exponential law, see Proposition
5.7
.
We now prove a recurrence result by giving a quantitative estimate of the
asymptotic number of entry times in a descending sequence of balls
. This is known as the
shrinking target problem
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Suppose that our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible.
Take
B_{\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega}}(p)
as a descending sequence of balls centered at the point
p
and with radius
\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega},
such that

E_{\omega,n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mu_{\sigma^{k}_{\omega},0}(B_{\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega}}(p))\rightarrow\infty\quad\mbox{ for $m$-a.e.\ $\omega$}.

Then for
m
-a.e.
\omega
and
\mu_{\omega,0}
-almost all
x,

T^{k}_{\omega}(x)\in B_{\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega}}(p)
for infinitely many
k
and

\frac{1}{E_{\omega,n}}\#\{k\geq 0,T^{k}_{\omega}(x)\in B_{\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega}}(p)\}\rightarrow 1.

The proposition is a simple consequence of the following
Borel-Cantelli
like property, whenever we put the observable
v
in the theorem below as
v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}=\mathbbold{1}_{B_{\xi_{\sigma^{k}\omega}}(p)}.

Theorem 7.5
.

Suppose that our random cocycle
\mathcal{R}=(\Omega,m,\sigma,\mathcal{B}_{\omega},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0})
is admissible. Take a nonnegative observable
v,
satisfying
\text{ess}\sup_{\omega}||v_{\omega}||_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\leq M.

Suppose that for
m
a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

E_{\omega,n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(x)d\mu_{\sigma^{k}_{\omega},0}(x)\rightarrow\infty,\ n\rightarrow\infty.

Then for
m
a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{E_{\omega,n}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(T^{k}_{\omega}x)=1,

for
\mu_{\omega,0}
-almost all
x
.

Proof.

Let us write

S_{\omega}:=\int\left(\sum_{m<k\leq n}\left(v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(T^{k}_{\omega}y)-\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}_{\omega},0}\right)\right)^{2}d\mu_{\omega,0}(y).

If we could prove that
S_{\omega}\leq\text{constant}\sum_{m<k\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0},

then the result will follow by applying Sprindzuk theorem
[
47
]
4
4

4

We recall here the Sprindzuk theorem. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{B},\mu_{0})
be a probability space and let
(f_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of nonnegative and measurable functions on
\Omega.
Moreover, let
(g_{k})_{k}
and
(h_{k})_{k}
be bounded sequences of real numbers such that
0\leq g_{k}\leq h_{k}.
Assume that there exists
C>0
such that

\int\left(\sum_{m<k\leq n}(f_{k}(x)-g_{k})\right)^{2}d\mu_{0}(x)\leq C\sum_{m<k\leq n}h_{k}

for
m<n.
Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
we have

\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}f_{k}(x)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}g_{k}+O(\Theta^{1/2}(n)\log^{3/2+\varepsilon}\Theta(n)),

for
\mu
a.e.
x\in\Omega,
where
\Theta(n)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}h_{k}.

, where we identify
\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0}
with the functions
g_{k}
and
h_{k}
in the previous footnote. So we have

\displaystyle S_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\sum_{m<k\leq n}v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(T^{k}_{\omega}y)\right]^{2}d\mu_{\omega,0}(y)-\left[\sum_{m<k\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}_{\omega},0}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m<k\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(T^{k}_{\omega}y)^{2}d\mu_{\omega,0}+2\sum_{m<i<j\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{i}\omega}(T^{i}_{\omega}y)v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}y)d\mu_{\omega,0}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{m<k\leq n}\left[\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0}\right]^{2}-2\sum_{m<i<j\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{i}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{i}\omega,0}\int v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}.

We now discard the third negative piece and bound the first by Hölder inequality as

\sum_{m<k\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}(T^{k}_{\omega}y)^{2}d\mu_{\omega,0}\leq\sum_{m<k\leq n}M\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0}.

Then for the remaining two pieces we use the decay of correlations given in Theorem 2.21 of our paper
[
4
]
, where the observables are taken in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
and in
L^{1}(\mu_{\omega,0}).
We can apply it to our case since the two observables coincide with
v_{\omega}
, and the measures
\mu_{\omega,0}
and
\nu_{\omega,0}
are equivalent. We point out that this result improves the decay bound given in item (
6
) of Theorem
6.11
where the presence of holes for
\varepsilon>0
forced us to use
L^{\infty}(\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon})
instead of
L^{1}(\nu_{\omega,\varepsilon}).

Hence:

\left|\int v_{\sigma^{i}\omega}(T^{i}_{\omega}y)v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}(T^{j}_{\omega}y)d\mu_{\omega,0}-\int v_{\sigma^{i}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{i}\omega,0}\int v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}\right|\leq CM\kappa^{j-i}\int v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}.

In conclusion, we get

\displaystyle S_{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m<k\leq n}M\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0}+CM\sum_{m<i<j\leq n}\kappa^{j-i}\int v_{\sigma^{j}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{j}\omega,0}

\displaystyle\leq M\left(\frac{C}{1-\kappa}+1\right)\sum_{m<k\leq n}\int v_{\sigma^{k}\omega}d\mu_{\sigma^{k}\omega,0},

which satisfies Sprindzuk.
∎

8.
Examples

In this section we present explicit examples of random dynamical systems to illustrate the quenched extremal index formula in Theorem
5.5
.
In all cases,
Theorem
6.11
can be applied to guarantee the existence of all objects therein for the perturbed random open system (in brief, the thermodynamic formalism for the closed random system implies a thermodynamic formalism for the perturbed random open system).
In Examples
1
–
3
we derive explicit expressions for the quenched extreme value laws.

Our examples are piecewise-monotonic interval map cocycles
whose transfer operators act on
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}=\text{{\rm BV}}:=\text{{\rm BV}}([0,1])
for a.e.
\omega
.
The norm we will use is
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}=\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}}:=\text{{\rm var}}(\cdot)+\nu_{\omega,0}(|\cdot|)
.
In Section
6
we noted that (
(B)
) is automatically satisfied.
Lemma
6.8
shows that (
(CCM)
) holds under assumptions (
E2
), (
E3
), (
(E4)
), and (
E8
).
To obtain a random open system, in addition to (
(B)
) and (
(CCM)
) we need (
(MC)
) (implying (
(M1)
) and (
(M2)
)), (
(A)
), and (
(X)
).
Therefore, in each example we verify conditions (
(A)
), (
(X)
) (Section
3.1
) and (
(MC)
) and (
E1
)–(
(E9)
) (Section
6
); and where required (
(S)
).

8.1.
Example 1: Random maps and random holes centred on a non-random fixed point (random observations with non-random maximum)

We show that nontrivial quenched extremal indices occur even for holes centred on a non-random fixed point
x_{0}
.
To define a random (open) map, let
(\Omega,m)
be a complete probability space,
and
\sigma:(\Omega,m)\to(\Omega,m)
be an ergodic, invertible, probability-preserving transformation. For example, one could consider an irrational rotation on the circle.
Let
\Omega=\cup_{j=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}
be a countable partition of
\Omega
into measurable sets on which
\omega\mapsto T_{\omega}
is constant.
This ensures that (
(MC)
) is satisfied.
For each
\omega\in\Omega
let
\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}=[0,1]
and let

T_{\omega}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathcal{J}_{\sigma\omega,0}
be random, with all maps fixing
x_{0}\in[0,1]
.
The observation functions
h_{\omega}:\mathcal{J}_{\omega,0}\to\mathbb{R}
have a unique maximum at
x_{0}
for a.e.
\omega
.

To make all of the objects and calculations as simple as possible and illustrate some of the underlying mechanisms for nontrivial extremal indices we use the following specific family of maps
\{T_{\omega}\}
:

(8.1)

T_{\omega}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1-2x/(1-1/s_{\omega}),&\hbox{$0\leq x\leq(1-1/s_{\omega})/2$;}\\
s_{\omega}x-(s_{\omega}-1)/2,&\hbox{$(1-1/s_{\omega})/2\leq x\leq(1+1/s_{\omega})/2$;}\\
1-(2x-(1+1/s_{\omega}))/(1-1/s_{\omega})&\hbox{$(1+1/s_{\omega})/2\leq x\leq 1$,}\end{array}\right.

where
1<s\leq s_{\omega}\leq S<\infty
and
s\rvert_{\Omega_{j}}
is constant for each
j\geq 1
; thus (
(M1)
) holds.
These maps have three full linear branches, and therefore Lebesgue measure is preserved by all maps
T_{\omega}
; i.e.
\mu_{\omega,0}=\mathrm{Leb}
for a.e.
\omega
.
The central branch has slope
s_{\omega}
and passes through the fixed point
x_{0}=1/2
, which lies at the centre of the central branch; see Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Graph of map
T_{\omega}
, with
s_{\omega}=2
.

A specific random driving could be
\sigma:S^{1}\to S^{1}
given by
\sigma(\omega)=\omega+\alpha
for some
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Q}
and
s_{\omega}=s^{0}+s^{1}\cdot\omega
for
1<s^{0}<\infty
and
0<s^{1}<\infty
, but only the ergodicity of
\sigma
will be important for us.
Setting
g_{\omega,0}=1/|T^{\prime}_{\omega}|
it is immediate that (
E1
), (
E2
), (
E3
), and (
(E4)
) hold.

We select a measurable family of observation functions
h:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
.
For a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,
h_{\omega}
is
C^{1}
, has a unique maximum at
x_{0}=1/2
, and is a locally even function about
x_{0}
.
For small
\varepsilon_{N}
we will then have that
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}=\{x\in[0,1]:h_{\omega}(x)>z_{\omega,N}\}
is a small neighbourhood of
x_{0}
, satisfying (
(E5)
) and (
(A)
).
In light of Remark
6.3
and Proposition
6.4
we see that (
(X)
) holds.
The corresponding cocycle of open operators
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}
satisfy (
(MC)
).
Given an essentially bounded function
t
, the
z_{\omega,N}
are chosen to satisfy (
(S)
). Because the
h_{\omega}
are
C^{1}
with unique maxima and
\mu_{\omega,0}
is Lebesgue for all
\omega
we can make such a choice of
z_{\omega,N}
with
5
5

5

In the situation where
z_{\omega,N}
is constant a.e. for each
N\geq 1
, in general we cannot satisfy (
(S)
) for a given fixed scaling function
t
. In the case where
t
is also constant a.e. if
h_{\omega}
is random but sufficiently close to a non-random observation
h
for each
\omega
, we can satisfy (
(S)
) by incorporating the error term
\xi_{\omega,N}
. This situation corresponds to a decreasing family of holes whose length fluctuates slightly in
\omega
with the fluctation decreasing to zero sufficiently fast. A similar situation can occur if the scaling function is a general measurable function. These situations motivate the use of the error term
\xi_{\omega,N}
in condition (
(S)
).

\xi_{\omega,N}\equiv 0
.
Furthermore, as
\mu_{\omega,0}
is Lebesgue we have that (
(S)
) implies that assumption (
E6
) is automatically satisfied.
With the above choices, (
E7
) is clearly satisfied for
n^{\prime}=1
.
To show condition (
E8
) with
n^{\prime}=1
we note that

\|g_{\omega,0}\|_{\infty}=(1-1/s_{\omega})/2<(1-1/S)/2
, while

\inf\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\mathbbold{1}\geq 2\cdot(1-1/s_{\omega})/2\geq 1-1/s
.
Therefore we require
(1-1/S)/2<1-1/s
, which by elementary rearrangement is always true for
0<s<S<\infty
.
Because there is a full branch outside the branch with the hole, (
(E9)
) is satisfied with
n^{\prime}=1
and
k_{o}(n^{\prime})=1
.

At this point we have checked all of the hypotheses of Theorem
6.11
and we obtain that for sufficiently small holes as defined above, the corresponding random open dynamical system has a quenched thermodynamic formalism and quenched decay of correlations.
We note that this result does not require (
(S)
); the holes
H_{\varepsilon_{N}}
do not need to scale in any particular way with
N
, they simply need to be sufficiently small.
In order to next apply Theorem
5.5

we do require (
(S)
) and additionally (
(C8)
).

To finish the example, we verify (
(C8)
).
We claim that for each fixed
k>0
, one has
\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(k)}=0
for sufficiently sufficiently large
N
(sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{N}
).
If this were not the case, there must be a positive
\mu_{\omega,0}
-measure set of points that (i) lie in
H_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
, (ii) land outside the sequence of holes
H_{\sigma^{-(k-1)}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}},\ldots,H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
for the next
k-1
iterations, and then (iii) land in
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
on the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
iteration.
In this example, because all
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
are neighbourhoods of
x_{0}
of diameter smaller than
(|t|_{\infty}+\gamma_{N})/N
, and the maps are locally expanding about
x_{0}
, for fixed
k
one can find a large enough
N
so that it is impossible to leave a small neighbourhood of
x_{0}
and return after
k
iterations.
For
k=0
, by definition

(8.2)

\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(0)}=\frac{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}=\frac{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\mu_{\omega,0}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}.

There are two cases to consider:

Case 1:

H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
is larger than the local preimage of
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
; that is,
T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\subset H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
. Because of the linearity of the branch containing
x_{0}
, one has
\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(0)}=1/T^{\prime}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(x_{0})
.

Case 2:

H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
is smaller than the local preimage of
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
; that is,
T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\supset H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
.
By (
8.2
) and (
(S)
) we then have

\frac{t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}-\gamma_{N}}{t_{\omega}+\gamma_{N}}\leq\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(0)}\leq\frac{t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}+\gamma_{N}}{t_{\omega}-\gamma_{N}}

and thus for such an
\omega
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(0)}=t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}/t_{\omega}
.

Thus, combining the two cases,

\hat{q}^{(0)}_{\omega,0}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{q}^{(0)}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}=\min\left\{\frac{t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}}{t_{\omega}},\frac{1}{|T^{\prime}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(x_{0})|}\right\}

exists for a.e.
\omega
, verifying
(
(C8)
)
.

Recalling that
\theta_{\omega,0}=1-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\hat{q}^{(k)}_{\omega,0}=1-\hat{q}^{(0)}_{\omega,0}
, we may now apply Theorem
5.5
to obtain the quenched extreme value law:

(8.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\nu_{\omega,0}\left(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\theta_{\omega,0}\,dm(\omega)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\left(1-\min\left\{\frac{t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}}{t_{\omega}},\frac{1}{|T^{\prime}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(x_{0})|}\right\}\right)\,dm(\omega)\right)

This formula is a generalisation of the formula in Remark 8
[
35
]
, where we may create nontrivial laws from either the random dynamics
T_{\omega}
, or the random scalings
t_{\omega}
, or both.
The following two special cases consider these mechanisms separately.

(1)

Random maps, non-random scaling (
t_{\omega}
takes a constant value
t>0
):
Since
|T_{\omega}^{\prime}|>1
, in this case (
8.3
) becomes

(8.4)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\nu_{\omega,0}\left(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon_{N}}\right)=\exp\left(-t\left[1-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|T_{\omega}^{\prime}(x_{0})|}\ dm(\omega)\right]\right),

and we see that we can interpret
\theta=1-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|T_{\omega}^{\prime}(x_{0})|}\ dm(\omega)
as an extremal index.

(2)

Fixed map, random scaling:

Suppose
T_{\omega}\equiv T
; then we may replace
T_{\omega}^{\prime}(x_{0})
with
T^{\prime}(x_{0})
in (
8.3
),
and we see we the extremal index depends on the choice of random scalings
t_{\omega}
alone; of course the thresholds
z_{\omega,N}
depend on the chosen
t_{\omega}
.

Similar results could be obtained with the
T_{\omega}
possessing nonlinear branches.
The arguments above can also be extended to the case where
x_{0}
is a periodic point of prime period
p
for all maps; the formula (
8.3
) now includes
(T_{\omega}^{p})^{\prime}(x_{0})
and
t_{\omega},t_{\sigma^{1}\omega},\ldots,t_{\sigma^{-(p-1)}\omega}
.
If the scaling
t_{\omega}=t
is non-random, one would simply replace
T^{\prime}_{\omega}(x_{0})
in (
8.4
) with
(T_{\omega}^{p})^{\prime}(x_{0})
.

We recall that for deterministic
T
(including non-uniformly hyperbolic maps), the extremal index enjoys a dichotomy, in the sense that it is equal to
1
when a single hole
H_{\varepsilon_{N}}
shrinks to an aperiodic point, and it is strictly smaller than
1
when the hole shrinks to a periodic point.
In the latter case the extremal index can be expressed in terms of the period; see
[
41
]
for a general account of the above facts.
Example
1
shows that in a simple random setting there are many more ways to obtain nontrivial exponential limit laws, e.g. by random scaling, or by the existence of periodic orbits for only a positive measure set of
\omega
.

8.2.
Example 2: Random
\beta
-maps, random holes containing a non-random fixed point (random observations with non-random maximum), general geometric potential

We show how a nontrivial extremal index can arise from random
\beta
-maps where statistics are generated by an equilibrium state of a general geometric potential.
Consider the “no short branches” random
\beta
-map example of Section 13.2
[
4
]
, where
\beta_{\omega}\in\{2\}\cup\bigcup_{2\leq k\leq K}[k+\delta,k+1]
for a.e.
\omega
, and some
\delta>0
and
K<\infty
.
The measurability of
\omega\mapsto\beta_{\omega}
yields (
(M1)
).
We use the weight
g_{\omega}=1/|T^{\prime}_{\omega}|^{r}
,
r\geq 0
.
To obtain (
(MC)
) the base dynamics is driven by an ergodic homeomorphism
\sigma
on a Borel subset
\Omega
of a separable, complete metric space, assuming that
\omega\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
has countable range, as in Example
8.1
.
Our random observation function
h:\Omega\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable and for a.e.
\omega
,
h_{\omega}
is
C^{1}
with a unique maximum at
x=0
.
We select a measurable scaling function
t_{\omega}
; either or both of
h_{\omega}
and
t_{\omega}
could be
\omega
-independent.
By assigning thresholds
z_{\omega,N}
(which could also be
\omega
-independent) we obtain a decreasing family of holes
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
, which are decreasing intervals with left endpoint at
x=0
.

Clearly (
E2
) and (
E3
) hold and Lemma 13.5
[
4
]
provides (
(E4)
).
Conditions (
(E5)
) and (
E6
), and (
(A)
) are immediate, as is (
E7
) with
n^{\prime}=1
. Condition (
(EX)
) holds because there is at least one full branch outside the branch with the hole.
Regarding (
E8
),
\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\beta_{\omega}^{-r}
and arguing as in the previous example,
since
\inf\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\mathbbold{1}\geq\lfloor\beta_{\omega}\rfloor/\beta_{\omega}^{r}
, we see (
E8
) holds with
n^{\prime}=1
because
\beta_{\omega}\geq 2
.
Because there is at least one full branch outside the branch with the hole, (
(E9)
) holds with
n^{\prime}=1
and
k_{o}(n^{\prime})=1
.
We have now checked all of the hypotheses of Theorem
6.11
.
By this theorem, for sufficiently small holes
H_{\varepsilon}
, the corresponding random open dynamical system has a quenched thermodynamic formalism and quenched decay of correlations.
As in the previous example, we emphasise that this result does not require (
(S)
).

Because
\varphi_{\omega,0}
is uniformly bounded above and below, as long as
z_{\omega,N}
is random, we may adjust
z_{\omega,N}
to obtain (
(S)
).
We now verify (
(C8)
) for our holes, which are of the form
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}=[0,r_{\omega,N}]
,
in preparation to apply Theorem
5.5
.
The same arguments from Case 1 and Case 2 of the previous example apply.
Case 2 is unchanged.
In Case 1 we have

\hat{q}^{(0)}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}=\frac{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}.

Because
T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
is a finite union of left-closed intervals, using the fact that
\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}\in BV
, and therefore has left- and right-hand limits everywhere, we may redefine
\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(y)
at the finite collection of points
y\in T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(0)
so that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\mu_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}=\frac{\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(0)}{\sum_{y\in T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(0)}\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(y)},

recalling that in Case 1,

T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\subset H_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
.
Taking the minimum of Cases 1 and 2 as in Example 1, we see that

\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(0)}
exists, which verifies (
(C8)
).
Finally,

(8.5)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\nu_{\omega,0}\left(X_{\omega,N-1,\varepsilon_{N}}\right)=\exp\left(-\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\left(1-\min\left\{\frac{t_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}}{t_{\omega}},\frac{\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(0)}{\sum_{y\in T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}(0)}\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}\omega,0}(y)}\right\}\right)\,dm(\omega)\right)

8.3.
Example 3: A fixed map with random holes containing a fixed point (random observations with a non-random maximum)

Let us now consider more closely the case of a fixed map and holes moving randomly around a fixed point
z
, a situation previously considered in
[
8
]
in the annealed framework.

We take a piecewise uniformly expanding map
T
of the unit interval
I
of class
C^{2}
, and such that
T
is surjective on the domains of local injectivity. Moreover
T
preserves a mixing measure
\mu
equivalent to the Lebesgue measure
\text{Leb},
with strictly positive density
\rho
.
We moreover assume that
T
and
\rho
are continuous at a fixed point
x_{0}
.
The observation functions
h_{\omega}
have a common maximum at
x_{0}
, leading to holes
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
that are closed intervals containing the point
x_{0}
for any
N
.
In Example
1
the holes were centred on
x_{0}
but could vary dramatically in diameter between
\omega
-fibers.
In this example the holes need not be centred on
x_{0}
but must become more identical as they shrink.
Specifically, we assume

(8.6)

\sup_{k}\frac{\text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\Delta H_{\sigma^{-k}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}\rightarrow 0,\ N\rightarrow\infty,

where the use of Lebesgue is for simplicity.

Since the sample measures
\mu_{\omega,0}
coincide with
\mu,

we may easily verify condition (
(S)
) by choosing conveniently the size of the hole
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
.
Moreover, by choosing the holes to be contained entirely within exactly one interval of monotonicity, we see that (
(EX)
) holds, and thus (
(X)
) holds via Remark
6.3
and Proposition
6.4
.
Since the weights
g_{\omega}
are nonrandom and equal to
1/|T^{\prime}|,
conditions (
E1
) to (
E7
) clearly hold.
Since
T
is continuous in
x_{0},
, the holes will belong to one branch of
T.
Therefore if
D(T)
will denote the number of branches of
T
and
\lambda_{m}:=\min_{I}|T^{\prime}|,

\lambda_{M}:=\max_{I}|T^{\prime}|,
it will be enough to have
D(T)-1>\frac{\lambda_{M}}{\lambda_{m}}
in order to satisfy (
(E9)
) with
n^{\prime}=1.
Moreover, still keeping
n^{\prime}=1
and since we have finitely many branches, condition (
(E9a)
) in Remark
C.2
is satisfied with
k_{o}(n^{\prime})=1
.
We may now apply Theorem
6.11
to obtain a quenched thermodynamical formalism for our fixed map with sufficiently small random holes.

Whenever the point
x_{0}
is not periodic, one obtains that all the
\hat{q}^{(k)}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
are zero by repeating the argument given in Example
1
for
k\geq 0
and using the continuity of
T
at
x_{0}
.
We now take
x_{0}
as a periodic point of minimal period
p
and we assume that
T^{p}
and
\rho
are continuous at
x_{0}
.
We now begin to evaluate

(8.7)

\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(p-1)}=\frac{\mu(T^{-p}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\mu(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}.

Since
T^{p}
is continuous and expanding in the neighborhood of
x_{0}
, by taking
N
large enough, the set
T^{-p}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}

has only one connected component.
Denote the local branch of
T
through
x_{0}
by
T_{x_{0}}
.
Therefore by a local change of variable we have for the upper bound of the numerator

(8.8)

\displaystyle\mu(T^{-p}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{T^{p}(T^{-p}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}\rho(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|DT^{p}(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|^{-1}d\text{Leb}(y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}).

For the lower bound of the numerator, since
T^{p}
is locally expanding,
T^{p}(H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})\supset H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
, and by (
8.8
)

\displaystyle\mu(T^{-p}H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})\geq\int_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\cap H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|DT^{p}(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y))|^{-1}d\text{Leb}(y)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\inf_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\inf_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})-\int_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\setminus H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|DT^{p}(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|^{-1}d\text{Leb}(y)

We can bound the second negative term as

\int_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\setminus H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|DT^{p}(T^{-p}_{x_{0}}y)|^{-1}(y)d\text{Leb}(y)\leq\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\ \sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\Delta H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}).

We are now in a position to verify the existence of the limit
\lim_{\varepsilon_{N}\to 0}\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(p-1)}
.
Using the above bounds we have

\frac{\inf_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\inf_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}-\frac{\ \sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}\Delta H_{\sigma^{-p}\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}

\leq\hat{q}_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}^{(p-1)}\leq\frac{\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}|DT^{p}|^{-1}\sup_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\ \text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}{\inf_{H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}}\rho\text{Leb}(H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}})}.

Since the map
T^{p}
and the density
\rho
are continuous at
{x_{0}},
and by using the assumption (
8.6
), we will finally obtain the
\omega
-independent value

\theta_{\omega,0}=1-\frac{1}{|DT^{p}({x_{0}})|}.

Theorem
5.5
may now be applied to obtain the quenched extreme value law.

8.4.
Example 4: Random maps with random holes

We saw in Examples
1
and
3
that an extremal index less than one could be obtained for observables reaching their maximum around a point which was periodic for all the random maps, or, for a fixed map, when the holes shrink around the periodic point.
We now produce an example where periodicity is not responsible for getting an extremal index less than one.
This example is the quenched version of the annealed cases investigated in sections 4.1.2 and 4.2.1 of the paper
[
15
]
.

Let
\Omega=\{0,\dots,l-1\}^{\mathbb{Z}},
with
\sigma
the bilateral shift map, and
m
an invariant ergodic measure.
To each letter
j=0,\dots,l-1
we associate a point
v(j)
in the unit circle
S^{1}
and we consider the well-known observable in the extreme value theory literature:

h_{\omega}(x)=-\log|x-v(\omega_{0})|,\ x\in S

where
\omega_{0}
denotes the
0
-th coordinate of
\omega\in\Omega.

In this case the hole
H_{\omega,\varepsilon_{N}}
will be the ball
B(v(\omega_{0}),e^{-z_{N}(\omega)}),
of center
v(\omega_{0})
and radius
e^{-z_{N}(\omega)};
.
For each
\omega\in\Omega
we associate a map
T_{\omega_{0}},
where
T_{0},\dots,T_{l-1}
are maps of the circle which we will take as
\beta
-maps of the form
6
6

6

The reason for this choice is that, in order to compute the quantities
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}
, we have to follow the itinerary of the points
v(i)
under the composition of the maps
T_{i}
and compare with their predecessors.
As it will be clear in the computation below for
k=0
and
1,

the task will be relatively easy
and generalisable to any
k>0
, if all the maps are at least
C^{1}
which in particular means that the image of the point
T_{i}(v_{j}),i,j=0,\dots,l-1
is not a discontinuous point of the
T_{i},i=0,\dots,l-1,
.
It will also be true that all the maps
T_{i},0=1,\dots,l-1
are differentiable with bounded derivative in small neighbourhoods of any
v_{i},i=0,\dots,l-1
.
If these conditions are relaxed, it could be that the limit defining the
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}
for some
k
is not immediately computable, we defer to section 3.3 and Proposition 3.4 in
[
6
]
for a detailed discussion of the computation of the extremal index in presence of discontinuities. Another advantage of our choice is that, as in Example
1
, all the sample measure
\mu_{\omega,0}
are equivalent to Lebesgue,
\text{Leb}.

T_{i}(x)=\beta_{i}x+r_{i}\pmod{1}
, with
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta_{i}\geq 3
, and
0\leq r_{i}<1
. Since the range of
\omega\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
is finite and the shift is a homeomorphism on
\Omega
with respect to the usual metrics for
\Omega
, assumption (
(MC)
) is verified.
Since the potential is equal to
1/|T_{\omega}^{\prime}|,
conditions (
E1
) to (
E7
) clearly hold.
Condition (
E8
) holds as in Example
1
, which uses similar piecewise linear expanding maps. Condition (
(E9)
) is a consequence of the fact that we have finitely many maps each of which is full branches; it will be therefore enough to invoke (
(E9a)
) with
n^{\prime}=k_{o}(n^{\prime})=1.

As we have chosen
\beta_{i}\geq 3
we have that (
(EX)
) holds and thus (
(X)
) follows via Proposition
6.4
.

At this point we may apply Theorem
6.11
to obtain a quenched thermodynamic formalism for sufficiently small holes.

As a more concrete example, we will consider an alphabet of four letters
\mathcal{A}:=\{0,1,2,3\}
and we set the associations
