i\rightarrow v_{i}:=v(i),\ i=0,1,2,3,

where the points
v_{i}\in(0,1)
, are chosen on the unit interval according to the following prescriptions:

(8.9)

T_{1}(v_{1})=T_{2}(v_{2})=v_{0};\ T_{0}(v_{0})=v_{3};\ T_{3}(v_{i})\neq v_{3},i=0,1,2,3.

Since the sample measures coincide with the Lebesgue measure, condition (
(S)
) reduces to

2e^{-z_{N}(\omega)}=\frac{t_{\omega}+\xi_{\omega,N}}{N}
and we can solve for
z_{N}(\omega)
by setting
\xi_{\omega,N}\equiv 0
.
The prescription (
8.9
) clearly avoids any sort of periodicity when we take the first iteration of the random maps needed to compute
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)};
nevertheless we will show that
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}>0
and this is sufficient to conclude that
\theta_{\omega,0}
is smaller than
1
.
Of course we need to prove that the limits defining all the other

\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}
for any
k>0
exist. We will show it for
k=1
and the same arguments could be generalized to any
k>1.

Using
T
-invariance of Lebesgue, we have (for simplicity we write
z_{N}
instead of
z_{N}(\omega)
):

\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\text{Leb}\left(T^{-1}_{\sigma^{-1}\omega}B(v(\omega_{0}),e^{-z_{N}})\cap B(v((\sigma^{-1}\omega)_{0}),e^{-z_{N}})\right)}{\text{Leb}(B(v(\omega_{0}),e^{-z_{N}}))},

provided the limit exists, which we are going to establish.
Consider first a point
\omega
in the cylinder
[\omega_{-1}=0,\omega_{0}=3]
; the quantity we have to compute is therefore

(8.10)

\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}=\frac{\text{Leb}\left(T^{-1}_{0}B(v_{3},e^{-z_{N}})\cap B(v_{0},e^{-z_{N}})\right)}{\text{Leb}(B(v_{3},e^{-z_{N}}))}

If
N
is large enough, by the prescription (
8.9
),
T_{0}(v_{0})=v_{3},
we see that the local preimage of
B(v_{3},e^{-z_{N}})
under
T_{0}^{-1}
will be strictly included into
B(v_{0},e^{-z_{N}})
and its length will be contracted by a factor
\beta_{0}^{-1}.
Therefore the ratio (
8.10
) will be simply
\beta_{0}^{-1}.
The same happens with the cylinders
[\omega_{-1}=1,\omega_{0}=0]
and
[\omega_{-1}=2,\omega_{0}=0],
producing respectively the quantities
\beta_{1}^{-1}
and
\beta_{2}^{-1}.
For all the other cylinders the ratio will be zero since the sets entering the numerator of (
8.10
) will be disjoint for
N
large enough. In conclusion we have

\int_{\Omega}t_{\omega}\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}dm=\int_{[\omega_{-1}=0,\omega_{0}=3]}t_{\omega}\beta_{0}^{-1}dm+\int_{[\omega_{-1}=1,\omega_{0}=0]}t_{\omega}\beta_{1}^{-1}dm+\int_{[\omega_{-1}=2,\omega_{0}=0]}t_{\omega}\beta_{2}^{-1}dm.

If we now take
t_{\omega}=t
,
\omega
-independent, and we choose
m
as the Bernoulli measure with equal weights
1/4
, the preceding expression assumes the simpler form

\int_{\Omega}t\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}dm=\frac{t}{16}(\beta_{0}^{-1}+\beta_{1}^{-1}+\beta_{2}^{-1}).

To compute
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(1)}
we have to split the integral over cylinders of length three. As a concrete example let us consider the cylinder
[\omega_{-2}=i,\omega_{-1}=j,\omega_{0}=k],
where
i,j,k\in\mathcal{A}.
By using the preceding notations, we have to control the set

T^{-1}_{i}T^{-1}_{j}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})\cap T^{-1}_{i}B^{c}(v_{j},e^{-z_{N}})\cap B(v_{i},e^{-z_{N}})=

T^{-1}_{i}\left(T^{-1}_{j}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})\cap B^{c}(v_{j},e^{-z_{N}})\right)\cap B(v_{i},e^{-z_{N}}).

Let us first consider the intersection
T^{-1}_{j}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})\cap B^{c}(v_{j},e^{-z_{N}}).
Call
u
one of the preimages of
T^{-1}_{j}(v_{k})
and
T^{-1}_{j,u}
the inverse branch giving
T^{-1}_{j,u}(v_{k})=u
. If
u=v_{j}
then the intersection
T^{-1}_{j,u}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})\cap B^{c}(v_{j},e^{-z_{N}})
is empty. Otherwise,
by taking
N
large enough, the set
T^{-1}_{j,u}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})
will be completely included in
B^{c}(v_{j},e^{-z_{N}})
and moreover we have, by the linearity of the maps,
T^{-1}_{j,u}B(v_{k},e^{-z_{N}})=B(u,\beta_{j}^{-1}e^{-z_{N}}).
We are therefore left with the computation of
T^{-1}_{i}B(u,\beta_{j}^{-1}e^{-z_{N}})\cap B(v_{i},e^{-z_{N}}).
If
u\in\mathcal{A}
we proceed as in the computation of
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}
by using the prescriptions (
8.9
); otherwise such an intersection will be empty for
N
large enough. For instance, if, in the example we are considering, we take
k=0,j=2,i=0
and we suppose that among the
\beta_{2}
preimages of
v_{0}
there is, besides
v_{0},

v_{3}
too, namely
T_{2}(v_{3})=v_{0},
and no other element of
\mathcal{A},

7
7

7

This condition does not intervene to compute
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(0)}
and show that it was strictly less than one.
then we get the contribution for
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(1)}:

\int_{[\omega_{-2}=0,\omega_{-1}=2,\omega_{0}=0]}t_{\omega}\beta_{2}^{-1}\beta_{0}^{-1}dm.

From the above it follows that
\theta_{\omega,0}
exists for a.e.
\omega
, and that we may apply Theorem
5.5
.

It is not difficult to give an example where all the
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}
can be explicitly computed. Let us take our beta maps
T_{i}(x)=\beta_{i}x+r_{i}
-
\text{mod}\ 1
in the particular case where all the
r_{i}
are equal to the irrational number
r.
Then take a sequence of random balls
B(v((\sigma^{k}\omega)_{0}),e^{-z_{N}}),k\geq 0
with the centers
v((\sigma^{k}\omega)_{0}),k\geq 0
which are rational numbers. From what we discussed above, it follows that a necessary condition to get a
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}\neq 0
is that the center
v((\sigma^{-(k+1)}\omega)_{0})
will be sent to the center
v(\omega_{0}).
Let
z
one of this rational centers; the iterate
T^{n}_{\omega}(z)
has the form
T^{n}_{\omega}(z)=\beta_{\omega_{n-1}}\cdots\beta_{\omega_{0}}z+k_{n}r,
-mod
1,
where
k_{n}
is an integer number.
Therefore such an iterate will be never a rational number which
shows that all the
\hat{q}_{\omega,0}^{(k)}=0
for any
k\geq 0
and
\omega,
and therefore
\theta_{\omega,0}=1.

Appendix A
A version of
[
22
]
with general weights

In this appendix we outline how to extend relevant results from
[
22
]
from the Perron–Frobenius weight
g_{\omega}=1/|T_{\omega}^{\prime}|
to the general class of weights
g_{\omega}
in Section
6
.
To begin, we note that there is a unique measurable equivariant family of functions
\{\varphi_{\omega,0}\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
guaranteed by Theorem 2.19
[
4
]
(called
q_{\omega}
there).
We wish to obtain uniform control on the essential infimum and essential supremum of
\varphi_{\omega,0}
for a suitable class of maps.

In
[
22
]
we work with the space
\text{{\rm BV}}=\{h\in L^{1}(\text{{\rm Leb}}):\text{{\rm var}}(h)<\infty\}
and use the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}}=\text{{\rm var}}(\cdot)+\|\cdot\|_{1}
.
Here we have a measurable family of random conformal probability measures
\nu_{\omega,0}
(guaranteed by Theorem 2.19
[
4
]
) and we work with the random spaces
\text{{\rm BV}}_{\omega}=\{h\in L^{1}(\nu_{\omega,0}):\text{{\rm var}}(h)<\infty\}
and the random norms
\|\cdot\|_{\text{{\rm BV}}_{\omega}}=\text{{\rm var}}(\cdot)+\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}(\nu_{\omega,0})}
.
We also work with the normalised transfer operator
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}(f):=\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}(f)/\nu_{\omega,0}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}\mathbbold{1})
; throughout Appendix
A
we simply denote
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega,0}
by
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}
as we only deal with the closed random system. Similarly we denote
g_{\omega,0}
,
\nu_{\omega,0}
, and
\varphi_{\omega,0}
by
g_{\omega}
,
\nu_{\omega}
, and
\varphi_{\omega}
respectively.
All of the variation axioms (V1)–(V8) in
[
22
]
hold with the obvious replacements.

6.8
.

Since

, (

) and (

We show

This is a generalised version of Lemma 2

).

All of the arguments go through as per

Full Support:

Following the proof of Claim 3.1.1 of

∎

We note that by (

)
with

(A.3)

\text{{\rm var}}(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{n}_{\omega}\varphi)\leq A\alpha^{n}\text{{\rm var}}(\varphi)+B\nu_{\omega}(|\varphi|),

for all

\omega

Define random cones

Lemma A.2

(General weight version of Lemma 1
[

]
)
.

If one has uniform covering in the sense of (11)
[

]

N
such that for each

such that

\essinf_{\omega}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}^{Nn}h\geq(c/2)\nu_{\omega}(|h|)\mbox{ for every $h\in\mathcal{C}_{\omega,a}$ and a.e.\ $\omega$}.

Proof.

Making all of the obvious substitutions, as per Lemma
A.1
and its proof, we subdivide the unit interval into an equipartition according to
\nu_{\omega}
mass.
This is possible because

]

-measure
1/n

Then using uniform covering and the facts that

,

, where

.
The rest of the proof follows as in
[

]
.
∎

Appendix B
A summary of checks that relevant results from
[
17
]
can be applied to
BV

The stability result Theorem 4.8
[
17
]
assumes that the underlying Banach space is separable, however this separability assumption is only used to obtain measurability of various objects (and in fact Theorem 3.9
[
17
]
may be applied to sequential dynamics).
We use these results for the non-separable space
BV
in the proof of Lemma
6.9
, and we therefore need to check that all relevant results in
[
17
]
hold for
BV
, under the
m
-continuity assumption on
\omega\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}
; the latter will provide the required measurability.
All section, theorem, proposition, and lemma numbers below refer to numbering in
[
17
]
.

There are no issues of measurability in Section 3, including Theorem 3.9, until Section 4, so we begin our justifications from Section 4.

Theorem 4.8:

This is the main stability theorem.
We would substitute “separable strongly measurable random dynamical system” with
m
-continuous random dynamical system. This theorem relies on Propositions 4.15 and 4.16, Lemma 4.21, Proposition 4.22, and Lemma 4.23.

Proposition 4.15:
There is no measurability involved.

Proposition 4.16:
Uses Lemmas 4.17 and 4.19. We note that the bounds (95)–(97) in the proof are simpler in our application of this result as our top space is one-dimensional.

Lemma 4.17:
This may be replaced by Theorem 17
[
29
]
, which treats the
m
-continuous setting. This removes any use of Lemma B.1 and Lemma B.7.

Lemma 4.18:
This uses Lemma 4.17 and the fact that compositions of
m
-continuous maps are
m
-continuous. The latter replaces the use of Lemma A.5
[
30
]
, which is used in several results. This replacement will not be mentioned further.

Lemma 4.19:
We will assume that
\omega\mapsto\Pi_{\omega}
is
m
-continuous in the statement of the lemma. At the start of the proof we would now instead have
\omega\mapsto\Pi_{\omega}(X)
is
m
-continuous by the definition of
m
-continuity (see e.g. (4) in
[
29
]
); this removes the use of Lemma B.2 in the proof.
Lian’s thesis is quoted regarding measurable maps/bases connected with a measurable space
\Pi_{\omega}(X)
.
In our application,
\Pi_{\omega}
has rank 1 and therefore stating that there is a (in our case
m
-continuous) map
e:\Omega\to\Pi_{\omega}(X)
is trivial. One proceeds similarly for the dual basis.

Lemma 4.21:
This concerns measurability and integrability of
\omega\mapsto\det(\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{n}|E_{i,\omega})
and
\omega\mapsto\log\|\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{n}|E_{i,\omega}\|
where
E_{i,\omega}
is an Oseledets space.

m
-continuity of
\omega\mapsto E_{i,\omega}
is provided by Theorem 17
[
29
]
, removing the need for Lemma B.2.
The
m
-continuity of
\omega\mapsto\log\|\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{n}|E_{i,\omega}\|
follows from Lemma 7
[
29
]
.
Because in our application setting we only require one-dimensional
E_{i,\omega}
, the determinants are given by norms and there is nothing more to do concerning determinants.
This removes the need for Proposition B.8.
Lemma B.16
[
30
]
may be replaced with Lemma 7
[
29
]
to cover the
m
-continuous setting.
Proposition B.6 is not required in the
P
-continuity setting.

Proposition 4.22:
There is no measurability involved.

Lemma 4.23:
There is no measurability involved.

Appendix C
A
var
–
\nu_{\omega,0}(|\cdot|)
Lasota–Yorke inequality

Recall from Section
6
that
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}
denotes the partition of monotonicity of
T_{\omega}^{n}
and that
\mathscr{A}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}
is the collection of all finite partitions of
[0,1]
such that

(C.1)

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}_{A_{i}}(g_{\omega,0}^{(n)})\leq 2\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}

for each
\mathcal{A}=\left\{A_{i}\right\}\in\mathscr{A}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}
.
Given
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{A}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}
, we set
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}:=\left\{Z\in\widehat{}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A}):Z\subseteq X_{\omega,n-1,\varepsilon}\right\}
where
\widehat{}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})
is the coarsest partition amongst all those finer than
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,0}^{(n)}
such that all elements of
\widehat{}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})
are either disjoint from
X_{\omega,n-1,\varepsilon}
or contained in
X_{\omega,n-1,\varepsilon}
. Then (
C.1
) implies that

(C.2)

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})\leq 2\|g_{\omega,0}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}

for each
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}
.
We now prove a Lasota–Yorke inequality inspired by
[
2
]
.

Lemma C.1
.

For any
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}(I)
we have

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f))\leq 9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+\frac{8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}{\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(A)}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f)=\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{n}(f\cdot\hat{X}_{\omega,n-1,\varepsilon})
, if
Z\in\widehat{}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(\mathcal{A})\backslash\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}
, then
Z\cap X_{\omega,n-1,\varepsilon}=\emptyset
, and thus, we have
\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}(f\mathbbold{1}_{Z})=0
for each
f\in\text{{\rm BV}}(I)
. Thus, considering only on intervals
Z
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}
, we are able to write

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f=\sum_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}}(\mathbbold{1}_{Z}fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}

where

T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}:T_{\omega}^{n}(I_{\omega,\varepsilon})\to Z

is the inverse branch which takes
T_{\omega}^{n}(x)
to
x
for each
x\in Z
. Now, since

\mathbbold{1}_{Z}\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}=\mathbbold{1}_{T_{\omega}^{n}(Z)},

we can rewrite (
C.3
) as

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f=\sum_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}}\mathbbold{1}_{T_{\omega}^{n}(Z)}\left((fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}\right).

So,

(C.5)

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f)\leq\sum_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}}\text{{\rm var}}\left(\mathbbold{1}_{T_{\omega}^{n}(Z)}\left((fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}\right)\right).

Now for each
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}
we have

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}\left(\mathbbold{1}_{T_{\omega}^{n}(Z)}\left((fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})\circ T_{\omega,Z}^{-n}\right)\right)\leq\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})+2\sup_{Z}\left\lvert fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 3\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})+2\inf_{Z}\left\lvert fg_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 3\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(f)+3\sup_{Z}|f|\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)})+2\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\inf_{Z}|f|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 3\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(f)+6\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\sup_{Z}|f|+2\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\inf_{Z}|f|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(f)+8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\inf_{Z}|f|

(C.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(f)+8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\frac{\nu_{\omega,0}(|f\rvert_{Z}|)}{\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}.

Using (
C.6
), we may further estimate (
C.5
) as

(C.7)

\displaystyle\text{{\rm var}}(\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}f)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}}\left(9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}_{Z}(f)+8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\frac{\nu_{\omega,0}(|f\rvert_{Z}|)}{\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 9\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\text{{\rm var}}(f)+\frac{8\|g_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}}{\min_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n)}(A)}\nu_{\omega,0}(Z)}\nu_{\omega,0}(|f|),

and thus we are done.
∎

Remark C.2
.

Note that we could have used
Leb
or any probability measure in (
C.6
) rather than
\nu_{\omega,0}
. Furthermore, Lemma
C.1
could be applied to Section
6
with a measure other than
\nu_{\omega,0}
if the appropriate changes are made to the assumption (
(E9)
) so that a uniform-in-
\omega
lower bound similar to (
6.18
) may be calculated.

In particular if we replace (
(E9)
) with the following:

(E9a)

There exists
k_{o}(n^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
such that for
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, all
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, we have
\text{{\rm Leb}}(Z)>\delta
for all
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}(\mathcal{A})
,

(E9b)

There exists
c>0
such that
\essinf_{\omega}|T_{\omega}^{\prime}|>c
,

then the claims of Section
6
hold with
\nu_{\omega,0}
in (
C.6
) replaced with
Leb
.

Indeed, to obtain a replacement for (
6.18
) one could use (
(E9a)
) and (
(E9b)
) to get

\displaystyle\text{{\rm Leb}}(Z)

\displaystyle=\text{{\rm Leb}}(\tilde{}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}\mathbbold{1}_{Z})\geq\frac{\inf g_{\omega,0}^{(k_{o}(n^{\prime}))}\inf J_{\omega}^{(k_{o}(n^{\prime}))}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}}\text{{\rm Leb}}(P_{\omega}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}\mathbbold{1}_{Z})

\displaystyle\geq\essinf_{\omega}\frac{\inf g_{\omega,0}^{(k_{o}(n^{\prime}))}\inf J_{\omega}^{(k_{o}(n^{\prime}))}}{\lambda_{\omega,0}^{k_{o}(n^{\prime})}}\text{{\rm Leb}}(Z)>0

for all
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\omega,*,\varepsilon}^{(n^{\prime})}(\mathcal{A})
, where we have also used (
E3
) for the final inequality.
As (
(E9)
) is only used to prove (
6.18
), the remainder of Section
6
can be carried out with the appropriate notational changes. In fact, the proof of Lemma
6.9
can be simplified by replacing
\nu_{\omega,0}
with
Leb
as Lemma 5.2 of
[
10
]
would no longer be needed.

Remark C.3
.

Note that the
2
appearing in (
C.1
), and thus the
9
and
8
appearing in (
C.7
), are not optimal. See
[
4
,
2
]
for how these estimates can be improved.

Appendix D
Proof of Claim (
6
) of Theorem
6.11

Proof of Claim (
6
) of Theorem
6.11
:

Define the fully normalized operator
\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\to\mathcal{B}_{\sigma\omega}
given by

\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f):=\frac{1}{\rho_{\omega,\varepsilon}h_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f\cdot h_{\omega,\varepsilon}).

Then we have that
\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}\mathbbold{1}=\mathbbold{1}
and
\mu_{\sigma\omega,\varepsilon}(\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}f)=\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(f)
.
Following the proof of Theorem 12.2
[
2
]
we can prove the following similar statement to Claim (
5
) of Theorem
6.11
: For each
h\in\text{{\rm BV}}
,
m
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(D.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}h-\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h)\mathbbold{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}=\|\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\leq D\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n},

where
\hat{h}:=h-\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h)
and
D
,
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
are as in Claim (
5
) of Theorem
6.11
.
Using standard arguments (see Theorem 11.1
[
4
]
) we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left(f\circ T_{\omega}^{n}\right)h\right)-\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h)\right\rvert=\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\right).

Note that at this stage we are unable to apply (
D.1
) as the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}
norm and the measure
\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}
are incompatible.
Now from the third statement of Claim (
5
) of Theorem
6.11
we have that

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\right)-\frac{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\hat{X}_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)}{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(X_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)}\right|\leq D\|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n},

and thus we must have that

(D.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\right)\leq D\|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}+\frac{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\hat{X}_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)}{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(X_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon})}.

Using (
D.1
) and (
6.14
), we have that

(D.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\hat{X}_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)}{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(X_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\infty}\|\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\leq D\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}.

Combining (
D.2
) and (
D.3
) and using (
D.1
) again we see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left(f\circ T_{\omega}^{n}\right)h\right)-\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(f)\mu_{\omega,\varepsilon}(h)\right\rvert\leq\mu_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq D\|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}+\frac{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}\left(\left|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\right|\hat{X}_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon}\right)}{\varrho_{\sigma^{n}\omega,\varepsilon}(X_{\sigma^{n}\omega,n,\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq D\|f\mathscr{L}_{\omega,\varepsilon}^{n}\hat{h}\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{n}\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}+D\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq D^{2}\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{2n}+D\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\tilde{D}\|f\|_{\infty,\omega}\|h\|_{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}\kappa_{\varepsilon}^{n}

for all
n
sufficiently large,
and thus the proof of Claim (
6
) of Theorem
6.11
is complete.

∎
